#### Abstracts
##### **Verify the Verifier: Specification-Grounded LLM Cross-Checking for Bugs That Escape UVM Scoreboards**
2611.26588v1 by Ana E. Sanchez Villalobos, Michael D. Quinn, Aakash Tyagi, Minh D. Luu, David Kebo Houngninou

A passing UVM regression shows the design agrees with the reference model on what the scoreboard checks, not that it meets the specification. Bugs that pass every test yet violate the specification, termed scoreboard escapes, survive mature testbenches. We present Verify the Verifier, to our knowledge the first framework whose LLM-based Anomaly Cross-Checker examines a passing regression's traces against the specification. Of 564 bugs inserted into four OpenTitan IPs, 68 are candidate scoreboard escapes: non-equivalent, exercised by the tests, yet passing. The framework confirmed 41 as scoreboard escapes with assertions failing on the buggy design and proven on the original.

摘要：

##### **The Geometry of Lies: Knowledge and Persona Directions for Detecting Deception in Large Language Models**
2611.26545v1 by Md. Abdullah Al Zami, Suraiya Shanaz, Mahdi Hasan, Shoaib Ahmed Dipu, Farig Yousuf Sadeque, Md. Sabbir Ahmed

Linear probes are the cheapest LLM lie detectors but often fail to transfer across kinds of lie. Label-free directions from honest and deceptive role-play are untested at 70B scale and on lies about knowledge, where a lie and an honest denial can be identical. We widen a published persona bank with roles that deny knowledge they have, plus honest counterparts. At each model's middle layer the resulting persona direction ranks lies across Liars' Bench better than single-dataset probes on three of its four models; it fails on Qwen, even when Qwen stays in role far more often. On Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct it beats directions from a random persona bank, but its gain over the published direction is within the range of random widenings. On lies about knowledge, persona directions mainly track a reply's wording: where lies and honest denials share their words, our three main contrasts stay inside the random-direction band on Llama and Qwen and reach only its edge on Gemma, though on Mistral one clears it. A knowledge direction contrasts questions the model can answer with ones nobody can, each followed by the same scripted denial. It separates lies from identical honest denials on two models and narrowly on a third, but not on the fourth; its other results partly depend on the benchmark's wording. On the Gender-Secret fine-tune it clears the band by a few hundredths on Llama and fails on Mistral. The sum of the two directions ranks lies better than a supervised probe at the same depth on all four models (on one, both near chance) but is level with the persona direction on Llama's average and behind that probe on held-out lies. More varied role-play questions do not help the persona direction on Liars' Bench.

摘要：

##### **Backtracking Agent Applications Security Incidents**
2611.26525v1 by Yicheng Cai, Taegyu Kim, Peng Liu

Agent applications are increasingly deployed, yet agent-specific attacks pose real threats to them. Upon a security incident, organizations backtrack to identify the CEP (compromise entry point), remediate the damage, and prevent its recurrence. The main artifacts available for backtracking are trajectory logs, which record agent functionalities, and source code; app(lication)-specific function call logs are often fairly limited. However, trajectory logs are at the agent functionality abstraction layer, while the CEP candidates are function calls at the app code execution layer. Due to this semantic gap, the analyst needs to manually inspect the relevant code for each CEP candidate to infer its corresponding agent functionality. In this work, we present Origin, a backtracking tool for the analyst to submit a small number of queries to identify CEPs that existing tools cannot provide. Our approach poses two challenges: (1) define the intermediate abstraction that captures the mapping relationships between the two semantic layers; (2) determine the region-to-variable mapping for constructing the cross-layer graph defined in the intermediate abstraction. Each region is a specific group of agent functionalities. Case studies on five popular agent applications across eight attack incidents show that an analyst using Origin correctly identifies all the CEPs.

##### **Auditing black-box clinical prediction models with a large language model through queries alone: a blinded test with planted flaws**
2611.24877v1 by Zhiyu Liu, Xinyu Yang

Large language models (LLMs) are used to explain clinical prediction models, on the promise that they can say what a model computes, find its faults and even reveal new knowledge in it. We tested the three promises where the answers could be checked. From the International Stroke Trial we trained four kinds of model (logistic regression, explainable boosting machine, gradient-boosted trees, neural network) for two outcomes on patients randomised earlier, planted one flaw in each of 24 copies (an under-predicted subgroup, a reversed risk factor or an implausible step) and mixed them with the 8 clean models. GPT-6 Astra audited each model three times, blind, through a tool that only returned the model's predictions; the judging rules were committed before any run and applied by program. Its surrogate models, with a median of 128 terms, reproduced the audited models' log-odds with a mean $R^2$ of 0.976 (0.959 for an additive surrogate of 42 terms fitted by an analyst). It found the planted flaw in 69 of 72 runs (96%; 95% CI 80% to 99%); a script written for the purpose found all 24. It also reported many more problems: 60% of 1,089 checkable reports failed the pre-specified check against the model's average behaviour; post hoc, most of these were real but small steps, and 15% of the checkable reports were not found in the model. Of 601 unexpected relationships it read out of the clean models, 11% held in patients randomised later (random pairs 6%; a regression scan's shorter list of its strongest candidates, 28%); after a check on the training data, 33% against 30%. With query access alone, an LLM can describe a black-box model faithfully and find nearly every planted flaw, as a purpose-written script also did; its reports of problems must be checked on the model and its discoveries on data.

##### **WorldRuler: Interactive World Modeling under Configurable Rules**
2611.24869v2 by Mingliang Zhai, Chuanhao Li, Jiaming Tan, Zhen Li, Yuwei Wu, Yunde Jia, Zian Meng, Yuanyang Yin, Kaipeng Zhang

Rules govern how player actions change interactive worlds. Different rules create distinct gameplay experiences within similar scenes and action spaces. For customizable gameplay, generative world models must follow user-defined rules beyond real-world behaviors. However, realizing user-defined rules presents two challenges: Rule-State Persistence and Rule-to-Visual Realization. The former requires preserving rule definitions, dependencies, and world states across saving and loading, so that restored worlds follow the established rules. The latter requires accurately realizing rule-induced changes on designated objects and attributes, particularly when affected objects are unseen. In this paper, we propose WorldRuler, which organizes rule definitions, dependencies, and object states in an executable 3D rule graph and connects rule execution, video generation, and perceptual feedback in a closed loop. The graph supports joint saving and restoration of rules and world states, while dynamic object binding grounds rule-specified entities in visual instances and uses stored states to constrain generation from their first appearance. We further introduce object attribute state replacement and counterfactual reconstruction training, enabling accurate attribute control and compatible appearance and motion changes while limiting interference with unrelated content. We develop an automated data collection and annotation pipeline using GTA-V and Habitat to construct RuleVerse, a dataset of rule-driven interactions with detailed state and event annotations, including cases where rules take effect before affected objects become visible. Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of WorldRuler in preserving rule-state consistency across saving and loading and accurately realizing rule outcomes on designated objects and attributes.

##### **Induction Head Origins of SLAM-ASR Speech Recognition Models**
2611.24721v1 by Artem Fedorchenko, Tanel Alumäe

What makes a small trained map from encoder states to the embedding space of a frozen language model sufficient for competitive speech recognition? The premise of the SLAM-ASR architecture is that a frozen language model possesses linguistic knowledge rich enough to perform the recognition task. This assumption, while reasonable, provides little insight into the mechanisms underlying these models. We therefore seek the concrete internal structures on which they rely. This work shows that SLAM-ASR models rely on induction heads of the frozen language model: the same heads that copy repeated token sequences in text are also important for mapping speech embeddings to transcript tokens. Moreover, we find pairs of heads that behave as a constrained form of differential attention, cancelling attention noise and sharpening attention onto the previously generated word. Finally, we show how induction heads could be used to improve transcription quality under noisy audio.

A central challenge in adapting large language models (LLMs) is capability preservation: an adaptation can achieve its target while degrading general capabilities. We introduce CurvDelta, a scalable framework that incorporates the preservation constraint into the parameterization of the model update. By analyzing the local geometry of the preservation constraint around the pre-adaptation model, CurvDelta constructs a low-curvature space in which parameter updates leave the preservation loss nearly invariant. We make its construction practical for LLMs through Kronecker-factored approximate curvature (K-FAC) and a factorized projection. We also introduce CurvLoRA, a low-rank variant within the same preservation geometry, designed for more efficient adaptation. We evaluate our methods on mathematical reasoning, code generation, and knowledge unlearning. For mathematical reasoning and code generation, our approaches achieve competitive downstream performance while better preserving general capabilities, offering a favorable acquisition--retention trade-off relative to the baselines. For knowledge unlearning, our main results on TOFU and MUSE show a better balance between targeted forgetting and knowledge retention than the baselines, with retention performance close to the from-scratch retraining reference.

##### **Late Entry in Looped Language Models**
2611.24576v1 by Ziqi Jin, Jiaxi Li, Weiwen Xu, Aixin Sun

Looped language models deepen reasoning at a compact parameter count by iterating a shared layer stack T times per token, turning recurrence depth directly into sequential decoding latency. Conventional early-exit decoding halts after initial passes without cache completion, truncating cached key-value states of past tokens to shallow depth. We study the opposite strategy, late entry, where new tokens skip initial passes and enter directly into the final K < T passes over a deep context cache. Once lightweight fine-tuning resolves hidden-state norm mismatch at the entry boundary, deep cached context substitutes for skipped token-local passes, allowing late entry to outperform matched early exit across model families and pass budgets. To turn this asymmetry into wall-clock speedups without stalling to build deep caches, candidate blocks verify through only the final K passes on the critical path while previously committed tokens complete passes 1 -> T alongside subsequent steps at zero extra serial calls. Projecting these finished T-pass wake states inside the existing output GEMM (Wake verification, governed by logit-margin tolerance tau in [0, inf]) unifies greedy-exact T-pass decoding (tau = 0, matching K = T block decoding within bf16 reduction order), margin-gated verification (tau > 0), and non-rollback lossy late entry (tau = inf) inside a single static CUDA graph. On Ouro-2.6B (T = 4), K = 2 Wake reaches 5.2x greedy-exact speedup at tau = 0 (+26% over K = 4 block decoding on GSM8K) and 6.8x at tau = inf (+16.9 points over trained early exit at matched speed). Scaling test-time recurrence across T in {8, 16, 32, 64} on Huginn-3.5B cuts K = T/4 rollbacks 21.5x with depth, driving greedy-exact (tau = 0) speedups to 6.1x (T = 32) and 8.4x (T = 64, +153% over K = 64 block) and tau = inf speedups to 10.6 to 12.5x.

Multimodal knowledge graph reasoning (MKGR) aims to infer missing facts by jointly exploiting relational structures, textual descriptions, and visual evidence. To address these challenges, we propose NaviGATE, a noise-aware generative framework for MKGR. In the noise-aware dual-view instruction tuning stage, NaviGATE jointly optimizes retrieval-free and retrieval-aware views: reliable retrieved candidates are reinforced through contrastive logit sharpening, while misleading candidates are suppressed through parametric consistency distillation. In inference, structure-constrained decoding restricts generation to valid entities in the target knowledge graph. Experiments on standard benchmarks show that NaviGATE consistently outperforms strong unimodal, multimodal, and generation-based baselines, and remains robust under noisy retrieval scenarios.

Mechanistic Interpretability (MI) seeks to decode neural network computations into human-understandable logic. 2611.24377v1 by Dongjun Han, Mingyu Lee, Ikjin Lee

Multi-fidelity Bayesian optimization (MFBO) reduces engineering design cost by combining inexpensive low-fidelity (LF) models with a few accurate high-fidelity (HF) evaluations and sequentially selecting designs to evaluate. However, when HF data are scarce, the LF-HF discrepancy is difficult to learn from numerical data alone, especially when physics omitted in the LF model makes it design-dependent. Yet even without sufficient data, domain experts often know which physics the LF model omits and under which conditions its effect grows. Existing multi-fidelity (MF) models cannot use such qualitative knowledge directly, but large language models (LLMs) can interpret it and compare designs accordingly. The proposed LLM-Assisted MFBO adds a knowledge basis to the conventional MF model: from an expert description of the omitted physics, an LLM judges which of two designs requires the larger signed residual correction, and a pairwise Gaussian process converts these judgments into the basis. On a lithium-ion battery design problem, the proposed method reduced the regret area under the curve of conventional MFBO by 61%. Benchmarks of figure-text verification typically hand the model the figure, the claim, or the knowledge that an inconsistency exists, which leaves untested whether a model can find the figure worth checking in a complete paper, relate a visual element to the claim it conflicts with, and recognize the conflict. We study this autonomous setting on 31 full manuscripts, each carrying a reviewer-documented figure-text inconsistency from PRISMM-Bench that is hidden from the model. Six multimodal models review each PDF under a neutral prompt, and we then add one input at a time to the same manuscripts. Of 1,445 open-ended reviews, 60.6% mention the flawed figure, yet a grading rubric accepts only 3.3% as diagnosing its contradiction; a blind audit finds the rubric lenient, so we read these as rubric-accepted rates and compare conditions rather than absolute values. In a separate controlled run, naming the figure, without stating that it is flawed, raises rubric-accepted diagnosis from 4.3% to 20.4%, while a consistency instruction and coarse visual cues do not. When the contradiction is visible only in the rendered figure, text-only review yields no accepted diagnosis, while review of the rendered pages with the figure named reaches 15.8%. A baseline that lets the model choose which figures to check, ranked by centrality, stays at 4.8%. Scientific problems have no answer key, so evaluating an agent means deciding what counts as a better solution before any agent is run. Existing benchmarks either match a symbolic answer against a key or, where they require executable work, score it by unit tests, by a rubric, or by position on a competition leaderboard. None of these is the criterion by which a field decides that one published method beats another. STEMBench adopts that criterion directly. Each of its twelve research problems in high-energy physics, astrophysics and neuroscience is scored by its own field's figure of merit, and each comes with two measured anchors: a submission that uses no domain knowledge, and an expert reference reproducing a published method, both obtained by running code shipped with the benchmark. The interval between them states how much of a problem is crossed by scientific insight rather than by engineering. Across eight frontier models, task-level wins and domain-level leadership dissociate: a model that wins five of the twelve tasks leads no domain mean. In the high-effort cross-model comparison, at least one model's mean score exceeds the expert anchor on seven of twelve tasks. The remaining five span uncertainty estimation, latent and causal inference, and cross-site clinical classification. Numerical scores and official-submission validity capture different aspects of performance and are reported separately. We release the tasks, graders, anchors and harness.

Building autonomous agents that continually explore open worlds and acquire new capabilities through experience is a long-standing aspiration in artificial intelligence. Existing research often pursues lifelong learning within predefined tasks, curricula, or objectives, with self-improvement largely confined to individual agents. We introduce OpenKAGE, an agent-group evolution framework for open-ended lifelong learning. Within this framework, agents autonomously explore open worlds and continually acquire new capabilities by accumulating and reusing knowledge from their own interactions and the experience of others. OpenKAGE builds on two key mechanisms: (1) a two-layer knowledge representation, with a memory layer for situated memories and an experience layer for transferable experience and skills that retains the applicability conditions needed for reuse; and (2) a knowledge-sharing mechanism that semantically consolidates textual experience into a continuously evolving shared knowledge graph, while executable skills are shared verbatim. 摘要：

##### **Colombo: A Frontier-to-Frontier Autoresearch System for Oracle-Based Optimization**
2611.23080v1 by Haihan Zhang, Wendao Wu, Chenheng Zhang, Chunyuan Zheng, Yanyi Li, Cong Fang, Haoxuan Li, Zhouchen Lin

Science advances from frontier to frontier as discoveries reshape what is known and create the basis for further discovery. We present Colombo, a self-organizing AI researcher that drives this renewal, first instantiated in oracle-based optimization. Here, the central question is how efficiently a problem class can be solved through a prescribed information interface and what costs no admissible algorithm can avoid. The gap between achievable guarantees and unavoidable limits defines the frontier recorded in its Complexity Atlas, a living map of frontier settings and the current state of knowledge. Self-Organization turns unresolved gaps into adaptive research programmes, coordinating conjecture formation, mathematical investigation, and replanning as evidence accumulates. Adversarial Co-Ascent develops algorithms and hard families through reciprocal challenge, exposing mechanisms of information use and concealment that guide stronger methods and proofs of unavoidable costs. Independent review and Lean-based formal certification check mathematical claims, while the Long-Term Memory carries supported findings and research experience across settings. To date, Colombo has established optimal or near-optimal rates for more than fifty settings in the Atlas. Colombo thus takes a concrete step toward Autopoietic Science: a scientific process whose validated advances regenerate the conditions of its own progress, continually reshaping how inquiry is organized and what it can discover.

摘要：

##### **ReTouchGS: Ultralight Render-free Aesthetic Color Enhancement and Transfer for 3D Gaussian Splatting**
2611.23054v1 by Chuanzhi Xu, Boyu Wei, Chengkun Yue, Xuanhua Yin, Yiyang Li, Haodong Chen, Qiang Qu, Weidong Cai

3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) enables photorealistic scene reconstruction. Existing 3DGS color transfer and interactive editing methods are guided by reference images and user-specified color adjustments. However, in many applications, users simply want aesthetically guided color enhancement that automatically makes the scene more visually appealing while maintaining consistent colors and tones across viewpoints. We present ReTouchGS, to our knowledge the first framework for reference-free aesthetic color enhancement of pretrained 3DGS models. It predicts a scene-level color lookup table (LUT) from the model and its associated camera parameters. ReTouchGS also supports reference-guided color transfer by fitting a scene-level LUT using one edited view of the same scene and its corresponding original render. A shared blend-aware color refinement network with only 44.7K parameters refines an analytic color update to realize either color mapping without rendering. It preserves geometry and delivers standard 3DGS assets in seconds. We also design the ReTouch3D benchmark to support the evaluation of aesthetic color enhancement and reference-guided color transfer across viewpoints. Experiments demonstrate high color accuracy and strong generalization to unseen scenes in both aesthetic color enhancement and reference-guided color transfer.

摘要：

##### **Can Decision Models Replace LLMs in Real-World Contact Center Classification and Judging Tasks?**
2611.22949v1 by Md Tahmid Rahman Laskar, Xue-Yong Fu, Shashi Bhushan TN

Contact center systems often need to make bounded decisions, such as identifying a caller's intent, retrieving the knowledge-base article that answers a query, assessing agent compliance, or judging the output of AI models used in production. Decision models return a typed answer and its probability directly, making them a promising cost-effective alternative to generative Large Language Models (LLMs) in such bounded conversational tasks. However, their effectiveness in real-world contact center applications remains unclear. In this paper, we compare nine decision models against thirteen generative LLMs in zero-shot settings across eight contact center use cases: six classification tasks and two judging tasks. Our experiments demonstrate that many decision models can match the performance of frontier LLMs on contact center classification, and API-based closed decision models achieve this at the lowest per-request cost among the API models we evaluate. We further observe that their confident predictions are more accurate, enabling selective routing in real-world production scenarios. Across classification tasks, the main weakness of most decision models is abstention: they rarely choose the no-answer option, which accounts for most of their gap to the strongest LLMs (although Gemini-Decision-1 is the notable exception in this regard). We also find that the judging quality of decision models drops sharply for smaller models, and they are also more prone to position bias. In short, our findings provide practical guidance for selecting decision models based on abstention behavior, confidence, and deployment cost, rather than overall accuracy alone.

摘要：

##### **Better Rankers, Worse Retrievers: The Hidden Cost of RL Post-Training in Generative Recommendation**
2611.22838v1 by Yuheng Chen, Fan Zhang, Yixuan Xu, Chao Yang, Yang Xiang, Can Yang

Generative recommenders decode the semantic ID of a user's next item with a language model, and post-training them with reinforcement learning (RL) after supervised fine-tuning (SFT) has become standard practice. The gains are mostly reported on the first ten results, yet in deployment such models hand hundreds of candidates to a downstream ranker or reward model. We show that RL post-training has a hidden cost: it improves accuracy at the top of the list (hit@K for small K) but, on all five datasets we study, lowers the recall of the candidate list (hit@K for K in the hundreds). The model becomes a better ranker and a worse retriever. Controlled experiments trace the loss to the negative advantages that RL assigns to the candidates it samples, the rollout. The root cause is that only one next item is logged per user, so RL treats every other candidate as wrong. Yet these candidates are the model's most plausible alternatives, and often the next items of other users with similar histories. Penalising them pushes down exactly what similar users choose next, and these items drop out of those users' candidate lists. This diagnosis points directly to a remedy: out-of-rollout anchoring, a single additional loss term that preserves the SFT model's ordering of the items outside the rollout. It keeps the gain at the top and restores recall to the SFT level on all five datasets, and it is effective under other RL configurations as well. To our knowledge, this is the first systematic study of this recall loss: we measure it, identify its cause, and show that, once the cause is known, a simple remedy suffices.

摘要：

##### **A tiny object detection dataset for high-altitude open-water search and rescue**
2611.22825v1 by Hui Xu, Chenfei Hou, Henglai Wei, Hao Wang, Yidong Jin, Xiulin Niu, Haoyang Zhang, Hui Zhang, Tianshi Xu

Large aerial platforms often operate above 1.5 km to improve the efficiency of open water search and rescue (OW-SAR). However, long imaging distances, wide fields of view, and limited sensor resolution reduce objects to a few pixels, increasing their susceptibility to environmental interference and making detection harder than for conventional small objects. To our knowledge, existing datasets focus on low-altitude scenarios and do not support automatic detection at 1.5-2.0 km. We therefore present TinyWOD, a tiny object detection dataset for high-altitude OW-SAR using lifebuoys as detection targets. It comprises 11,359 manually annotated images and 19,505 annotated instances spanning diverse environments, lighting conditions, water colors, and surface ripples. Compared with existing datasets, TinyWOD contains much smaller objects and a substantially higher proportion of tiny instances; its average equivalent side length (ESL) is 12.7 pixels, with 76.96% of in stances having ESLs below 15 pixels, reflecting actual high-altitude water-rescue imaging conditions. We define APtiny, a tiny object detection metric, and benchmark mainstream and state-of-the-art networks using this and conventional metrics. All evaluated networks effectively detect tiny waterborne objects, demonstrating TinyWOD's reusability and utility. We expect this dataset to support research on tiny object detection in high-altitude OW-SAR.

摘要：

##### **Pluggable Subband-Decoupled Fusion and Unlabeled Capture Prototype Calibration: Bridging High-SNR Null-Subcarrier Regrowth and Low-SNR Coherent Preambles for Cross-Domain IoT RF Fingerprinting**
2611.22812v1 by Fengchong Yao, Jianbing Li, Qing Liu, Kefeng Song, Haitao Li, Song Wang, Kunpeng Wang

Zero-overhead physical-layer authentication at edge wireless access points (APs) is vital for securing resource-constrained Internet of Things (IoT) transceivers against credential cloning, yet cross-domain IEEE 802.11a/g radio frequency fingerprint identification (RFFI) faces an acute signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) crossover. Power-amplifier spectral regrowth on unpopulated null subcarriers resists indoor multipath at high SNR but decays quadratically below the thermal noise floor, whereas populated active in-band preambles survive low SNR yet degrade under severe multipath, causing static fusion to lose up to 44.7 percentage points (pp). To our knowledge, we propose the first RFFI framework that dynamically modulates active in-band waveforms and unpopulated null-subcarrier regrowth via a blind physical SNR gate: Pluggable Gated Subband-Decoupled Fusion with Unlabeled Capture-Aware Prototype Calibration (GSDF-CAPC). Avoiding the 20-35 dB subband gradient imbalance of joint training, GSDF pairs any frozen pretrained host backbone with a 46.3K null-subcarrier plug-in encoder (NS-Branch), fusing scale-standardized logits and gate-warped spherical embeddings under a source-calibrated lag-64 thermal-SNR gate with exact low-SNR fallback. Over unlabeled target AP captures, forward-only unbalanced optimal-transport calibration (UOT-CAPC) aligns shifted prototypes under packet-arrival skew and recovers parameter-free balanced Sinkhorn (U-CAPC) at equipartition. Across DataPort-WiFi, WiSig, and ORBIT MobRFFI, zero-retrain GSDF wins all 72/72 paired runs across eight backbones with 0.00 pp low-SNR loss, while edge IB-Host + GSDF (92.6K-93.3K active parameters, 1.82 ms single-core CPU latency) and timing-synchronized GSDF-STO / GSDF-Tri reach 57.50%-65.05% three-benchmark Macro-F1 (89.04%-100.00% over 5-10-packet IoT bursts).

摘要：

##### **Proximal Targets: Training Looped Refiners on Problems with Many Correct Answers**
2611.22737v1 by Syed Affan Daimi

Looped refiners such as HRM and TRM apply one small network repeatedly to a draft answer, and deep supervision trains every pass toward one stored answer per puzzle. We prove that when a puzzle has many correct answers this target sets a loss floor that no model capacity removes, and that the loss-minimising pass blends the answers into an invalid grid. We propose the proximal target: train each pass toward the correct answer nearest to its own input, found by scanning a list of answers, by a search solver started at the draft, or within a band of quality set by a knob; correct answers become fixed points, and a pass only has to make fewer errors than its input has for the loop to converge, which its training loss bounds. With the same 1.7M-parameter network and 5 seeds, the proximal target solves 98.8% of Sudoku puzzles with 2-64 answers (standard target: 2.5%), 93.8% of sparse puzzles with more than 10,000 answers without any list of answers (0.0%), and 90.9% of 200-node 3-colourings after training on graphs of at most 50 nodes (36.9%), and different random starts end at different answers. Against GRAM, a stochastic-latent reasoner, a single run is correct far more often (98.8% vs. 57.7%) and twenty runs find more of the answers (53.6% vs. 37.4%), while GRAM is slightly ahead on its own 10-node colouring benchmark; in Scrabble the knob becomes a calibrated strength dial.

摘要：

##### **Stress Testing a Multimodal Large Language Model Grading Assistant on Kinematics Graph Reading**
2611.22726v1 by Ons Zammel, Carlos Soares, Flávia Carvalhido, Gerd Kortemeyer

Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can recognize graph features yet award credit without sufficient graphical support. This exploratory study examines selected grading outputs for synthetic responses to four kinematics exercises, generated through visual distortion and progressive transformation toward reference graphs. Different model and prompt configurations were used. Some judgments correctly distinguished instantaneous zero velocity from rest over a finite interval and recognized positive curvature. Yet a severely distorted velocity--time graph received full credit despite disconnected and branching traces and an unclear zero-velocity reference. Other submissions graded under the same configuration lost substantial credit. Numerical cutoffs intended to distinguish meaningful features from drawing irregularities were supplied without a coordinate convention for unnumbered graphs, leaving the basis of exact partial-credit awards unresolved. Stress testing can expose unsupported awards before classroom use. For the configurations examined, we do not recommend accepting scores as final grades without instructor review, including review of full-credit responses.

摘要：

##### **Visibility Graph Representations of Word Embeddings for Text Classification**
2611.22685v1 by Elif Dorukbaşı, İlker Türker

Text classifiers depend on representations that expose the distinctions between classes in a form the learning model can use, and a restricted vocabulary changes which representation is most effective. This paper studies natural visibility graphs as an intermediate representation for news topic classification under controlled vocabulary budgets. Word embeddings are averaged into a fixed-length document vector, the vector is read as an ordered numerical series, and its natural visibility graph is passed to a classifier either as an ordered node-degree vector or as an adjacency matrix. On the four-class AG News benchmark, chi-square selection retains 100 to 1,500 words, and the graph inputs are compared with direct embedding-based representations from continuous bag-of-words, skip-gram, GloVe, FastText and BERT. With a fully connected classifier, the degree representation is the most accurate configuration from 700 selected words onward; it reaches its highest accuracy of 91.24% at 1,000 words and exceeds the BERT-based baseline by 0.29 percentage points at 1,500 words. In a separate convolutional comparison, the adjacency input reaches 86.16% at 1,000 words, against 83.12% for the strongest static embedding. The results indicate that visibility-derived features can be competitive once the selected vocabulary provides adequate coverage. We discuss the roles of graph compression, coordinate ordering and classifier choice, and the controls needed to establish the reliability of the small differences observed.

摘要：

##### **CULypso: A Clustered Federated Unlearning paradigm for Vehicular Ad-Hoc Networks**
2611.22682v1 by Eleftheria Chini, George Margaritis, Dimitrios Katsaros

Unlearning has emerged as an essential security and privacy-preserving solution in Machine Learning, enabling networks to support their clients' rights for data erasure, as mandated by data protection regulations. Introducing such a non-trivial task to an edge, highly mobile and time-sensitive vehicular network is an even greater, largely unexplored challenge. This article argues that a hierarchical Federated Unlearning approach could be aptly integrated into vehicular ad-hoc networks (VANETs), leveraging topology and movement to reduce communication, resource and time costs, while effectively forgetting a client's influence on the global model updates. In order to achieve this, we propose CULypso, an approximate, surgical, hierarchical unlearning algorithm tailored for clustered VANETs. Inspired by the nymph in Greek mythology, Calypso, whose name translates to 'she who conceals the knowledge', CULypso, with the capital letters referencing \textbf{C}lustered \textbf{U}n\textbf{L}earning, aims to achieve exactly what its name suggests: conceal, erase or forget in our case, knowledge it contains. Extensive experiments uphold the potency of our paradigm with it performing approximately 65\% better than FedEraser in terms of time, server storage load, and unlearning fidelity while utterly outperforming baseline full retraining.

摘要：

##### **AfriNORM: An Evaluation Benchmark for Normative Reasoning of LLMs Across African Cultures**
2611.22580v1 by Ahmad Samuel Gali, Tadesse Destaw Belay, Idris Abdulmumin, Seid Muhie Yimam, Shamsuddeen Hassan Muhammad

Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed across various sectors. However, current evaluation benchmarks are insufficient to assess the applicability of these models in local African contexts, as most focus on linguistic fluency (grammar/translation) rather than cultural competence. The few existing benchmarks that measure general knowledge or social conventions do not capture the African deontic modality, i.e., the strict norms and taboos that govern diverse social interactions. In this paper, we introduce AfriNORM, a scenario-based, multiple-choice question dataset designed to measure and diagnose the normative reasoning limitations of LLMs when applied to African sociocultural contexts. We introduce two metrics: a Universal Commonsense Bias Score (UCBS) to quantify commonsense bias in LLM and a Distractor Bias Score (DBS) to quantify an LLM's limited understanding of African cultural norms. We also conduct a case study on the Yorùbá language, identifying confusion gaps in two closed-source LLMs, "Gemini 2.0 Flash" and "GPT-5", and one open-source LLM, "Llama 3.1 8B Instruct" when prompted in English versus Yorùbá. While their accuracy in English is high, their performance in Yorùbá is much lower, attributable to cultural confusion rather than simple bias, indicating that the models exhibit semantic confusion when reasoning about norms in Yorùbá.

#### Abstracts
##### **Active Feature Acquisition by Distilling an Oracle Teacher**
2611.26598v1 by Yang Meng, Zixin Ding, Robert L. Grossman, Yuxin Chen

Acquiring every feature of an instance is often costly: a measurement can carry a monetary price, a time delay, or a privacy cost. Active Feature Acquisition (AFA) addresses this by learning a policy that decides, per instance, which features to acquire under a budget. Existing AFA methods train this policy by optimizing a self-constructed utility target rather than distilling an explicit teacher. We construct this teacher explicitly. At training time the label and the unobserved feature values are available, and together they determine the teacher in closed form: an oracle that ranks every candidate by its effect on the downstream predictor's error. OracleImitator distills this ranking into a deployable policy by soft-target imitation. Across multimodal medical diagnosis and the AFABench benchmark, OracleImitator matches or outperforms existing AFA methods across the budget range, recovering 90% of the attainable accuracy gain at under a quarter of the acquisition cost. Because the teacher is an explicit algorithmic object rather than an implicit optimization target, we can also measure directly how much of its ranking is recoverable by a policy restricted to what is visible at deployment.

摘要：

##### **GEOHunter: Mapping a Generative Engine Optimization Network in the Wild**
2611.26564v1 by Sean Tang, Peiran Wang, Ying Li, Esha Shivakumar, Tao Meng, Yuan Tian

Generative engines synthesize retrieved web pages into answers, recommendations, and rankings. Generative engine optimization (GEO) services sell visibility in these answers by publishing promotional articles on user-generated-content (UGC) platforms for paying clients. Such promotion raises concerns in medical and financial domains, where misleading advice can cause harm. To study the severity of this problem, we measure one such paid-content operation across three major Chinese UGC platforms, uncovering 1,985 accounts associated with the network comprising 466,357 inferred promotional placements. We find that the network promotes products across diverse categories through a growing pool of accounts, conducting coordinated campaigns spanning months across multiple platforms while evolving its listicle content over time. Across nine generative engines from both the US and China, we find that 4.5% to 20% of retrievable cited pages are attributable to the network, depending on how strictly attribution is drawn. On eight of these engines, its clients are named in 52% of answers, against 68% for category leaders. Our work demonstrates the effectiveness of UGC GEO placement at influencing generative engine outcomes, and reveals content-level defenses as insufficient against coordinated UGC placements, where account-level and platform-level signals are necessary for proper defense.

摘要：

##### **Multi-institutional grading of Barrett's esophagus dysplasia without stain normalization using a pathology foundation model and multiple instance learning**
2611.26562v1 by Amirali Khosravi, Jaidip M. Jagtap, D. Chamil Codipilly, Catherine E. Hagen, Jason T. Lewis, Rickey E. Carter, Shahriar Faghani, Vani J. A. Konda, Fouad Otaki, Shajan Peter, Nicholas Shaheen, Sachin Wani, Prasad G. Iyer, Bradley J. Erickson

Barrett's esophagus dysplasia grading suffers substantial inter-observer variability, particularly for low-grade dysplasia, complicating clinical decision-making. We conducted a retrospective multi-institutional diagnostic accuracy study of a foundation model-based deep learning system for dysplasia grading, using 969 H\&E-stained whole slide images from four institutions. Patch-level classifiers trained on expert annotations identified diagnostically relevant regions, followed by attribute-aware multiple instance learning for slide-level classification as non-dysplastic Barrett's esophagus (NDBE), low-grade dysplasia, or high-grade dysplasia. On a held-out test set (n = 130), macro-averaged AUC was 0.96 (95\% CI 0.93--0.98). For binary dysplasia detection, sensitivity was 98\% (80/82) and specificity 81\% (39/48), with a negative likelihood ratio of 0.030 and quadratic-weighted \kap{} of 0.83 against expert consensus. No high-grade dysplasia case was misclassified as NDBE. Performance was consistent across institutions without stain normalization, indicating that stain-robust foundation-model representations may remove the site-specific preprocessing that has constrained prior systems to institution-specific deployment.

##### **DeepQuest: From Knowledge Gaps to Scientific Discovery in Alzheimer's Disease**
2611.26343v1 by Chunbo Jiang, Yifei Chen, Beining Wu, Fuyou Mao, Weihong Chen, Enyu Bao, Mingxuan Liu, Feiwei Qin, Qiyuan Tian

Automating experiments does not necessarily advance scientific understanding. Research agents must identify meaningful knowledge gaps, determine what available data can resolve, and assess whether their findings advance existing knowledge. We introduce DeepQuest, a knowledge-gap-driven AI scientist framework for Alzheimer's disease research. DeepQuest transforms unresolved questions from domain reviews into traceable, testable hypotheses and prioritizes them through data feasibility screening and groupwise Elo ranking. It couples iterative experimentation with two-level auditing that separates experimental reliability from knowledge contribution relative to two frozen literature corpora. On OASIS-3, DeepQuest produces 42 feasible hypotheses and outperforms the strongest baselines in 14 of 15 evaluator-dimension comparisons across three language-model reviewers. The full framework achieves an audit-assessed scientific finding rate of 95.24%, outperforming both general-purpose executors and domain-specific research agents. This scientific finding rate remains stable across literature corpora and evaluator model families. Representative findings concern neuropsychiatric symptoms and dementia risk, longitudinal amyloid spatial stability, and neocortical tau-associated clinical impairment, with key associations supported in independent ADNI and AIBL cohorts. These results show how aligning knowledge gaps, data feasibility, and evidence auditing can turn automated research into traceable, evidence-supported scientific discovery. Our source code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/DeepQuest-3066/

摘要：

##### **Verifiable Logic in Medical LLMs: A Critical Review of Agentic Clinical Reasoning Optimization and Neuro-Symbolic Grounding**
2611.25898v1 by Youssef ElNahas, Adel Ammar, Soumaya Chaffar, Wadii Boulila

Clinical large language models are usually judged by whether the final answer is right. Yet a model can reach a fluent, plausible output through invalid reasoning that static benchmarks do not detect, and confident hallucination, causally invalid inference, and spurious demographic shortcuts can pass conventional evaluation. This critical review covers four paradigms in turn: how clinical hallucination arises and why static metrics miss it; the agentic hallucination cascade, in which multi-agent and tool-augmented systems propagate and amplify early reasoning errors; process reward modeling as step-level supervision; and neuro-symbolic grounding, in which formal logic engines, ontology reasoners, and knowledge graphs place verifiable constraints on neural output. Within their encoded domains, systems with symbolic veto layers offer guarantees that hold relative to their encoded knowledge, and they report large gains on the safety measures they use. These results come from single studies, mostly in simulated or small-sample settings, and are not directly comparable with neural baselines. Process reward models remain bounded by the statistics of their training data. We propose a conceptual framework that maps each agentic failure mode to a symbolic intervention point, and we argue that clinical AI safety depends substantially on system architecture. Because current regulation emphasizes performance and does not require verification of reasoning steps, we argue that responsible certification should also audit reasoning trajectories alongside accuracy on static benchmarks.

摘要：

##### **Harnessing Medical Visual-Language Models with Agentic Evidence Management**
2611.25840v1 by Qiming Huang, Hanze Dong, Jipeng Zhang, Mike Zheng Shou, Xinxing Xu, Furu Wei

Medical vision-language agents increasingly rely on segmentation tools, and recent work has focused on acquiring the right mask, often treating a good mask as the end point of tool use. But do these agents use the resulting evidence effectively? To answer this question, we need a benchmark that separates evidence use from segmentation quality, spans multiple medical image types, and allows the same mask to be presented in different ways. Existing medical VQA datasets do not jointly provide these properties. We therefore construct Mask-Evidence MedVQA, a 2.5K-example benchmark spanning four image types, where each question is paired with an answer-relevant ground-truth mask and multiple possible renderings. Our study reveals two limitations of direct tool use. First, evidence presentation matters. Different renderings of the same fixed mask can substantially change model behavior. Second, masks introduce an evidence trade-off. Highlighting a relevant region can expose diagnostic evidence the model previously missed, but the added visual cue can also induce incorrect associations. As a result, even a ground-truth mask can correct some answers while turning initially correct ones into incorrect ones. Together, these findings show that acquiring the right mask is not enough. An agent must also decide how to present the evidence and whether it should change the answer. We therefore introduce TUNE (Tool Use is Not Enough), an evidence-management harness that explicitly manages what evidence to acquire, how to present it, and whether to accept the resulting answer update. Across three public medical VQA benchmarks, evidence management improves the fine-tuned Qwen3.5-9B backbone's mean accuracy by 4.0 percentage points. Full TUNE improves over direct tool use by 9.1 points on average across seven benchmarks and achieves competitive results against existing medical VLMs and agents.

摘要：

##### **From Reliance to Overreliance: Taxonomizing and Detecting Relational Risks in AI Companionship**
2611.25734v1 by Viet Cuong Nguyen, Fiona Li, Chang Wan, Sheyril Agarwal, Nhi Nguyen, Angel Hsing-Chi Hwang, Munmun De Choudhury

Companion AIs are increasingly used for emotional support, yet distinguishing meaningful engagement from harmful reliance remains difficult. We introduce the Taxonomy of Relational Risks (TRR), which operationalizes users' emotional overreliance (EO) as observable conversational behaviors and separately identifies evidence of relational harm (RH), enabling empirical examination of when reliance behaviors co-occur with adverse consequences. Using naturalistic Character.AI and Replika conversations from 110 adults, we found EO-related behaviors in 63.2% of non-roleplay conversations, while observable relational harms appeared in 10.5%. Although EO did not necessarily lead to harm, recurring patterns often co-occurred with harmful outcomes, suggesting value as risk indicators. Open-source LLMs satisfactorily detected 12 of 14 EO behaviors in at least one configuration. Qualitative analysis revealed expectations of care, repeated support seeking, dependency, distress, and reduced confidence in personal judgment. We argue for graduated safety interventions tailored to patterns and trajectories of EO-related behavior.

##### **Erasing the Wrong Thing: Conditional Attribute Leakage and Concept Erasure in Shared Foundation-Model Embeddings**
2611.25640v1 by Haijun He, Fei Li, Donghong Ji, Junjun He

Medical-image embeddings are increasingly shared in place of raw images for authorized downstream tasks, yet they may reveal sensitive attributes. Standard concept-erasure methods target population-level guardedness. However, we prove that, when binary task and sensitive labels are associated, global and task-conditional linear guardedness cannot both hold while a nonzero linear task signal is preserved, so global erasure must either eliminate that signal or retain task-conditional linear leakage. We further show how global erasure affects linear readers' discrimination between positive and negative samples with different sensitive attributes, deriving an approximate closed-form prediction of its utility cost from pre-erasure statistics. Motivated by this conflict, we introduce Task-Conditional LEACE (TC-LEACE), a closed-form variant of LEACE that enforces linear guardedness within each task stratum of its fitting data. We evaluate four vision foundation models on 85 CelebA attribute pairs spanning a broad range of associations. After LEACE, conditional leakage persists across all four encoders, the discrepancy between marginal and conditional audits increases with association strength, and predicted utility costs track observed losses. We then examine hospital chest radiographs using three foundation models, including two biomedical models, in which chest-tube status is strongly associated with diagnosis. Although a marginal attack on LEACE-erased embeddings underperforms a diagnosis-only reference, chest-tube status remains detectable within diagnosis strata. TC-LEACE recovers most of LEACE's linear task-utility loss while keeping task-conditional leakage within a prespecified margin of LEACE's.

##### **An Explainable and Scalable Hierarchical Tensor Framework for Integrating Multi-Omics and Digital Pathomics**
2611.25584v1 by Ahmad Hussein, Ali Anaissi, Mukesh Prasad, Ali Braytee

Background: Precision oncology requires integration of heterogeneous molecular and pathology data, yet many fusion models offer limited biologically grounded interpretability and struggle with missing modalities.
  Methods: We present Hierarchical Interpretable Tensor Fusion (HIT-Fuse), integrating six modalities: somatic copy number variation, DNA methylation, RNA sequencing, microRNA expression, RPPA protein abundance, and vision transformer-derived pathomics across four TCGA cohorts (glioma, lung adenocarcinoma, breast, and kidney cancer). Modality-specific autoencoders compress high-dimensional inputs, followed by two-stage CP tensor decomposition. Missing latent modalities are reconstructed using clinical covariates and observed omics to expand cohorts and retrain downstream models. A mathematical traceback mechanism enables global and local xAI attributions.
  Results: HIT-Fuse identifies distinct latent subgroups with significant survival stratification in glioma, breast, and kidney cohorts, both with complete and reconstructed modalities. Cohort expansion yields cohort-specific gains, including a 56.4% relative increase in weighted F1 for glioma. Biological validation against DepMap CRISPR screens, miRTarBase and RPPA annotations supports decoded drivers across all four cohorts. Essential gene rates range from 6.2% in glioma to 7.6% in lung cancer; example drivers include ANAPC11, SNRPD1, PLK1 and RPL14. Of decoded miRNAs, 71-92% have miRTarBase regulatory support, while 77-84% of selected genes are covered in DepMap.
  Conclusions: HIT-Fuse combines hierarchical tensor fusion, missing-modality reconstruction and traceable attributions to support interpretable multi-omics and digital pathology-based cancer prognosis across heterogeneous cohorts.

##### **Bilateral Evidence Selection and Reasoning for Mammography Vision--Language Models**
2611.25418v1 by Zhengbo Zhou, Dooman Arefan, Shandong Wu

Medical vision--language models (VLMs) often need to identify small, subtle lesions within high-resolution images containing extensive normal anatomy and redundant visual information. Visual-token compression improves efficiency, but preserving salient regions alone can discard the context needed for diagnostic interpretation. This context can extend across images: in bilateral mammography, the opposite breast provides a patient-specific anatomical reference. Yet retaining such evidence does not ensure that the model uses it effectively. We propose Bilateral Evidence Selection and Reasoning (BESR), which connects evidence preservation with comparative assessment through a bilateral-guided token selector and a bilateral reward. Under a shared token budget, the selector jointly prioritizes lesion appearance and its local and bilateral context, while the reward encourages assessments that benefit from the preserved comparison. Experiments on a bilateral dataset constructed from CBIS-DDSM and on VinDr-Mammo demonstrate improved evidence retention and diagnostic performance under constrained visual-token budgets. Inference benchmarking further shows reduced prefill latency.

##### **A Neurosymbolic Multimodal Framework for 30-Day Diabetic Readmission Prediction: Integrating Structured EHR Data, Dense Clinical Embeddings, and Knowledge-Grounded Symbolic Extraction**
2611.25236v1 by Una Suman Kumar Patro, Sibangi Subhadarsani, Narendra Badam

Unplanned 30-day readmissions among patients with diabetes mellitus impose a substantial clinical and economic burden, yet most predictive models use only structured electronic health record (EHR) data and ignore free-text clinical notes, which often contain code-mixed Hindi-English (Hinglish) phrasing and negation that general-purpose language models parse unreliably. We propose a neurosymbolic multimodal framework evaluated on the public Diabetes 130-US Hospitals dataset (101,766 encounters; 71,518 patients) with a patient-level grouped 80/20 split. Three streams are fused: engineered structured features; 32 components of Bio_ClinicalBERT note embeddings obtained by truncated singular value decomposition; and symbolic entities extracted by an open-weight clinical language model within a three-stage hybrid retrieval-augmented generation pipeline (dense retrieval, sparse lexical retrieval, and cross-encoder re-ranking) with reflective negation auditing. A cost-sensitive XGBoost classifier with a decision threshold calibrated by Youden's J statistic produces the prediction. On the held-out test cohort (n = 20,153), the structured baseline achieved an area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUROC) of 0.6477, sensitivity of 0.5839, and Brier score of 0.1080. The full multimodal model achieved the highest sensitivity (0.6667; +8.28 percentage points), the best F2-score (0.3824), and the lowest Brier score (0.0977), at a slightly lower AUROC (0.6375) and specificity (0.5161). Shapley additive explanations showed that prior utilization dominates risk, with compressed narrative components among the top predictors. On a 500-note code-switched benchmark, grounded retrieval raised Hinglish symptom recall from 4.8% to 40.2% and precision from 3.1% to 45.2%, while reducing entity hallucinations from 1.74 to 0.00 per note.

##### **MADJEPA: Enhanced Multi-Stage Joint-Embedding Predictive Architecture for Medical Anomaly Detection**
2611.25194v1 by Ioana-Sabina Stoian, Florinel-Alin Croitoru, Vlad Hondru, Andreea Iuliana Ionescu, Andrei Paun, Radu Tudor Ionescu

Anomaly detection in medical images is a complex task, requiring the development of a robust model using only healthy patient data. Without access to labeled data, modern approaches typically employ self-supervised learning to obtain a model of normality. Since world models promise to build strong internal representations of their environment, we consider them as suitable candidates for the medical anomaly detection environment, namely medical investigations of healthy patients. To this end, we propose MADJEPA, a medical anomaly detector based on an enhanced multi-stage joint-embedding predictive architecture (JEPA). We design a multi-stage training pipeline to produce a robust detector: (1) image-based JEPA (I-JEPA) training aims to learn a strong representation of normal training data; (2) target encoder distillation aims to modulate over-generalization of I-JEPA representations to abnormal data; (3) pseudo-anomaly classification aims to calibrate anomaly predictions by learning a tight boundary around the normal data manifold. To demonstrate our design, we carry out comprehensive experiments on six datasets covering various medical image domains (e.g.~MRI, OCT, X-ray, histopathology), comparing our MADJEPA with numerous state-of-the-art methods. The results show that our carefully designed world model, MADJEPA, attains competitive results, ranking first on three datasets. Our ablation studies further confirm the utility of each training stage, indicating that I-JEPA alone is not sufficient to obtain competitive results in medical anomaly detection. We publicly release our code at https://github.com/Sabina085/madjepa.

##### **Auditing black-box clinical prediction models with a large language model through queries alone: a blinded test with planted flaws**
2611.24877v1 by Zhiyu Liu, Xinyu Yang

Large language models (LLMs) are used to explain clinical prediction models, on the promise that they can say what a model computes, find its faults and even reveal new knowledge in it. We tested the three promises where the answers could be checked. From the International Stroke Trial we trained four kinds of model (logistic regression, explainable boosting machine, gradient-boosted trees, neural network) for two outcomes on patients randomised earlier, planted one flaw in each of 24 copies (an under-predicted subgroup, a reversed risk factor or an implausible step) and mixed them with the 8 clean models. Its surrogate models, with a median of 128 terms, reproduced the audited models' log-odds with a mean $R^2$ of 0.976 (0.959 for an additive surrogate of 42 terms fitted by an analyst). It found the planted flaw in 69 of 72 runs (96%; 95% CI 80% to 99%); a script written for the purpose found all 24. It also reported many more problems: 60% of 1,089 checkable reports failed the pre-specified check against the model's average behaviour; post hoc, most of these were real but small steps, and 15% of the checkable reports were not found in the model. Of 601 unexpected relationships it read out of the clean models, 11% held in patients randomised later (random pairs 6%; a regression scan's shorter list of its strongest candidates, 28%); after a check on the training data, 33% against 30%. With query access alone, an LLM can describe a black-box model faithfully and find nearly every planted flaw, as a purpose-written script also did; its reports of problems must be checked on the model and its discoveries on data.

##### **When Does Continual Pre-Training Help? A Controlled Study of Dialectal Adaptation for Québec French**

Continual pre-training (CPT) is widely used to adapt pretrained language models to dialectal and non-standard language varieties, yet it remains unclear when this additional training is necessary, what drives its benefits, and whether aggregate evaluation scores adequately capture its effects. In this paper, we present a controlled study of CPT for Québec French language understanding across nine LLMs spanning different families and scales. Using a budget-matched protocol of 30M tokens per experiment, our results show that the benefit of CPT depends strongly on the starting model's existing competence in the target variety. When CPT is beneficial, the improvements are driven more by corpus composition than by data volume, with literary text accounting for most of the gain. In the frozen-backbone branch, features are augmented with region-of-interest information, predicted foreground volume, and segmentation confidence. In the adaptation branch, the final encoder stage is partially fine-tuned for classification, with an auxiliary segmentation loss to preserve segmentation-related information. Additional five-fold experiments showed that more complex configurations did not consistently improve classification performance. On the public validation leaderboard, our selected framework achieved an Overall score of 0.8056. In the official FLARE 2026 Task 2 final evaluation, our framework ranked first overall, achieving the highest DSC, NSD, and AUROC across all four testing tasks, including two hidden testing tasks. Local inference measurements also remained below the challenge limits of $60 \mathrm{s}$ per case and $4 \mathrm{GB}$ GPU memory. These results demonstrate the practical value of reusing segmentation representations for classification while maintaining a simple and efficient framework.

##### **Anticipating or Speculating? Making Futures Pay in AI Ethics**
2611.22268v2 by John P. Nelson

Philosophers have recently been trying to develop uniform decision rules for instances in which physicians' judgments diverge from the recommendations of medical artificial intelligence (AI) systems. I argue that this effort is fatally flawed because the performance and appropriate use of algorithmic recommendation systems by physicians are highly context-specific, empirical medical matters rather than epistemological ones. In such instances, general decision rules can be stipulated only to the extent that they abstract away from relevant, indeed determinative, contextual circumstances. The alleged problem of physician-AI disagreement, and others like it, play into longstanding disputes in technology ethics about the viability and utility of envisioning possible futures: is this useful "anticipation" or wasteful "speculation"? I suggest that the two may be distinguished. Anticipation explores plausible futures to help inform present actions under irreducible uncertainty. Speculation abstracts away uncertainty, developing formal solutions to stipulated circumstances whose relationship to the real present is often quite unclear. Efforts to develop crisp philosophical solutions to physician-AI disagreement, among other problems, fall into the latter category. Technology ethics should remember that fruitful futures thinking must both begin from and return to the messy, uncertain present.

##### **Not All Evidence Is Admissible: Evidence Governance for Tool-Augmented Medical Agents**
2611.22169v1 by Runxun Zhang, Onofrio Antonio Catalano, Jinqiao Wang, Jingwei Wei

Tool-augmented medical agents treat whatever their tools return as evidence. We call this evidence hijacking. $\mathcal{V}$-information explains the phenomenon: the reasoner has lost the option to ignore, so a tool output can carry negative usable information, and moving the decision of what to keep outside the reasoner restores that option. The theory further shows that part of any filtering gain comes merely from letting the reasoner see less, and only selection by content lets it see better. Building on this, we recast tool-augmented reasoning as evidence governance, which separates deciding what counts as evidence from the reasoner that answers, and propose context-independent evidence governance (CIEG), the first medical agent framework that rules, after acquisition, on whether each individual tool output may enter as evidence. Every tool output is written as a card that can be checked against the raw input; an independent checker that sees only the input and the cards decides which cards become evidence; and the reasoner answers from the admitted cards alone, with no training anywhere. On medical question answering, fundus glaucoma screening, and chest X-ray pneumonia classification, it achieves the highest accuracy among models of comparable scale, and significantly outperforms state-of-the-art tool-using agents and multi-agent systems built on the same backbone at lower cost.

Related work aligns frozen image and text encoders on natural images without paired data. On 3D CT, LightAlign surpasses models trained end to end on image-report pairs without finding-level labels: with only 200 pairs, LightAlign outperforms CT-CLIP on CT-RATE (0.754 vs. 0.733 AUROC), and with only 100 CT-RATE pairs, it does so on RAD-ChestCT, an out-of-distribution dataset from a different hospital. This paper proposes the Data-to-Wisdom Epistemic Architecture for AI (EAAI), a conceptual framework treating Data, Information, Knowledge, Understanding and Wisdom as five distinct but recursively interacting epistemic functions: observation, contextual interpretation, modeling and prediction, critical evaluation and informed judgment. Two observations motivate it: sequential readings of the Knowledge Pyramid obscure how these functions interact, and a targeted review of twelve Bayesian reference books finds that prior distributions are defined mathematically while "prior information" and "prior knowledge" are not consistently distinguished as epistemic categories. A Bayesian interpretation relates the likelihood, prior and posterior distributions, posterior prediction, model checking and decision analysis to the five functions, without limiting EAAI to Bayesian systems. Extending "prior" in an epistemic sense, the paper distinguishes five kinds of prior context that can shape the model, the likelihood, the prior, evaluation and action. Three worked applications -- quantitative financial risk, actuarial modeling in health insurance and agentic AI for medical reimbursement claims -- show how EAAI structures the analysis of model assumptions, evidence interpretation, validation, dependencies and decision consequences, separating the prediction of a risk, the appraisal of its reliability and the justification of action. It draws implications for AI and machine learning, including evaluation by function, treating intermediate model outputs as evidence open to challenge and separating generation from appraisal, and compares EAAI with work on DIKW, Bayesian workflow, AI evaluation and risk governance. Developing models that can interpret complex examinations and communicate their findings in the context of clinical decisions remains a major challenge. Here, we show that Neuro-VL, a vision-language model trained on nearly one million neuroimaging studies from routine clinical care, can interpret complete imaging examinations and engage in natural language conversations about their findings. Across internal and international datasets, Neuro-VL outperformed frontier general-purpose and specialized medical models in diagnosing, localizing, and describing abnormalities, as well as generating comprehensive radiology reports. The model also compared serial examinations to assess disease progression and more reliably resisted misleading clinical assumptions than leading frontier models from OpenAI and Google. These results extend health system learning beyond individual predictions to interactive image interpretation and longitudinal disease monitoring. They demonstrate how the collective clinical experience of health systems can be used to develop medical AI with specialized expertise and broad clinical capabilities.

摘要：

##### **A Cardiorespiratory Foundation Model Pretrained on Ten Physiological Signals for Perioperative and Critical Care**
2611.15004v1 by Cheol-Hui Lee, Wookyung Kim, Hyeonhoon Lee, Garam Lee, Da-in Eun, Hyung-Chul Lee

Arterial, venous, pulmonary artery and intracranial pressures and ventilator waveforms guide decisions in operating rooms and intensive care units, yet foundation models for physiological waveforms have been built almost entirely on the electrocardiogram and photoplethysmogram. We present CARMEN, a single encoder pretrained without labels on ten cardiorespiratory waveforms (2.27 million modality-hours). CARMEN accepts any combination of co-recorded signals from 10 s to 5 min, and a conditioning mechanism, LSCNorm, restores the absolute signal level that normalization removes; without it, the AUROC for intraoperative hypotension fell from 0.894 to 0.780. Across 19 tasks from Korean and US cohorts, the frozen encoder outperformed supervised models on 12 of 15 and three foundation models, one general-purpose and two for biosignals, on 14 to 17 of 19, with none exceeding it on any task. Given the electrocardiogram or photoplethysmogram alone, it matched or exceeded signal-specific foundation models in most comparisons, and it retained its performance when channels were missing.

#### Abstracts
##### **One Prior, Two Dynamics: Repurposing Video Diffusion Transformers for Self-Supervised Latent Action Learning**
2611.26617v1 by Yuxin Jiang, Wenqiang Sun, Jiaqi Li, Yuchao Gu, Lvmin Zhang, Ivor Tsang, Mike Zheng Shou

Videos reveal how the world changes, but rarely the actions that cause those changes. Latent action learning recovers such supervision from visual transitions, yet often learns the underlying dynamics largely from scratch. We ask whether a pretrained video generator can instead provide the prior for both inverse and forward dynamics. We introduce O$^{2}$LaF, built on a simple principle: one prior, two dynamics. We initialize both models from the same pretrained video DiT and adapt them to their distinct roles: a feed-forward transition encoder with learnable action queries for inverse dynamics, and a causal, action-conditioned video generator for forward dynamics. By controlling information access and optimizing both dynamics end-to-end through a compact bottleneck, O$^{2}$LaF learns transferable actions from unlabeled videos. Across navigation and robotic manipulation, O$^{2}$LaF sets a new state of the art in few-shot action probing and enables stronger zero-shot action transfer across visual contexts and embodiments. Beyond generative video priors, we further extend our framework to predictive representation priors such as V-JEPA2, achieving competitive performance. Our findings suggest a broader paradigm for latent-action learning: repurposing pretrained video models to discover transferable actions and predict their consequences.

摘要：

##### **AgentRoboCritic: Understanding the Capability of Frontier Agents on Direct Robot Control**
2611.26613v1 by Yiyang Du, Yuxuan Kuang, Qitao Zhao, Yixuan Wang, Zhanqiu Guo, Yi Xu, Xin Li, Wenbin He, Liu Ren, Chenyan Xiong

Frontier multimodal language agents such as GPT-6 Astra have demonstrated strong capabilities in controlling robots directly via low-level numerical actions without high-level primitives. However, existing benchmarks report only episode-level success rates, which show neither how these agents succeed nor where they fail. In this paper, we present a systematic action-level analysis of frontier agents on direct robot control across 12 manipulation benchmarks. We introduce AgentRoboCritic, an intent-grounded critique framework in which the policy agent states a local intent alongside each action, and a critic evaluates the action's outcome and failure mode against this intent. Our action-level analysis demonstrates the promising capabilities of frontier agents on direct robot control, including closed-loop recovery from local execution failures, reliable semantic grounding across diverse scenes, and strong zero-shot manipulation without task-specific adaptation. It also identifies their current limitations, including task-relevant physical execution bottlenecks such as grasping and alignment, and uneven recovery across failure modes. Finally, we validate our critic on a 400-action human-annotated benchmark and release a dataset of 12,844 annotated actions alongside a fine-tuned open-source critic, Muse-Glimmer-30B, whose failure-mode accuracy rises from 32.58% to 74.24%, enabling low-cost action-level analysis.

##### **SoK: Assessing the Evolution of LLM Capability in Software Vulnerability Research**

LLM-based agents are becoming increasingly capable at discovering, validating and patching vulnerabilities in real software, and vendors and researchers report that this is already changing how vulnerabilities are found and fixed. These claims, however, are scattered across papers, vendor reports and disclosures and are rarely comparable. We introduce a framework that rates these capabilities on an autonomy scale modeled on the levels of driving automation, and qualifies each claim by its correctness, its availability and the strength of its evidence. We use this framework to systematize the published evidence on LLM capability in vulnerability research. We find that agents discovered, validated, and patched vulnerabilities in real-world software without human intervention before the restricted frontier models of 2026, and that open-weight models subsequently attained comparable autonomy for discovery and validation. In 2026 the number of disclosed vulnerabilities more than doubled, with an increasing share rated critical or high, and severe bugs that had survived a decade or more in widely used code were reported several times more often than before. Yet the time between the stages of a vulnerability's life, from report to fix and from disclosure to attack, has not shifted noticeably so far. We grade the mitigations proposed in the literature with an explicit rubric and draw guidance for each group of stakeholders from the best-supported ones.

##### **PGX: Physically Grounded Execution for Long-Horizon Humanoid Loco-Manipulation**
2611.26596v1 by Kai Lu, Chenyang Ma, Ioannis Havoutis

Robotic vision-language-action (VLA) models can execute diverse individual manipulation skills, yet they still struggle with long-horizon loco-manipulation tasks, where temporal structure, skills, and precision are tightly coupled within a monolithic model. We propose Physically Grounded Execution (PGX), a framework that offloads temporal and precision control to two classical training-free components, allowing the VLA to focus on stage-specific skill dynamics. Shared predicate semantics connect offline demonstration segmentation, online execution, and stage-wise evaluation; (ii) VLA skills operate within active manipulation phases, with flat or stage-trained models concentrating on task-relevant actions; and (iii) world-coordinate locking (WCL) maintains spatial consistency through base station keeping, object-relative positioning, and world-anchored contact targets. We demonstrate the effectiveness of PGX with extensive experiments conducted on three simulated tasks and one real-world task using a Unitree G1 humanoid robot for long-horizon loco-manipulation, showing improvements in stage progression and task success rates. Our critically damped Polyak (CDP) stepsizes multiply the classical Polyak rule by $4$ and a mild damping factor. CDP thus operates at the edge of stability by adapting its stepsizes to local curvature. Once the loss is small, its projected iterates follow gradient descent on $\log{λ_1(\nabla^2 \mathcal{L})}$ along $\mathcal{M}$ to leading order, paralleling the flatness bias of normalized gradient descent (Arora et al., 2022). Unlike normalized gradient descent, CDP step lengths shrink with the distance to $\mathcal{M}$, enabling convergence to an interpolating solution that locally minimizes sharpness at finite learning rate. We establish our theory for a broad class of non-negative losses with locally quadratic Morse-Bott structure near $\mathcal{M}$, capturing regular interpolation regimes in smooth overparameterized neural networks. Empirically, CDP stepsizes produce a flattening effect similar to that of Sharpness-Aware Minimization (SAM, Foret et al., 2021), reducing sharpness even in settings beyond our theory, including nonlinear teacher-student networks and fine-tuning of pre-trained LLMs.

##### **Monte Carlo Pass Search for Setpieces**
2611.26464v1 by Andrew Kang

Set pieces produce about a third of non-penalty expected goals (xG), yet they are usually evaluated by what happened next. We present Monte Carlo Pass Search (MCPS) for set pieces, which treats a corner or a lofted free kick as a distribution over plausible executions and plausible futures instead of a single realised event. From the pre-kick state, MCPS proposes counterfactual deliveries and counterfactual set-ups (where the attackers stand at the kick), plays each out many times in a world model (3D ball physics, a generative model of all 22 players, the keeper's come-or-stay decision and a Player-to-Touch model) and scores every future with a value model. On 249 deliveries from 30 Serie A 2025/26 matches, with every model applied only to matches it never saw, the best available delivery is worth 0.0085 xG more than the one kicked (+28 %), mainly because it reaches an attacker before the keeper. Searching where the attackers stand as well adds +0.0027 xG with the observed ball and +0.0033 on top of the best ball; designing their runs too adds +0.0007 more. We show how to keep such a search honest, and estimate how much more data each component needs before a club could rely on it.

##### **Flow Matching from Non-Gaussian Sources: A Theory of Efficient Sampling**
2611.26407v1 by Yuchen Zhou, Lili Zheng, Yuxin Chen, Gen Li

Flow matching is a versatile framework for generative modeling that transports a source distribution to a target data distribution. Conversely, router-ticket hierarchies incur lossy prose handoffs, heavy routing overhead, and blind merges - collapsing true-conflict repository merges to a 33.3% three-run mean despite high isolated branch passes. Across three programmatic multi-stream benchmarks and three model families, our portable trial merge gate lifts true-conflict merges to 63.3%, while ClonAct's routerless virtualization attains this ceiling with 45.9% fewer prompt tokens and prevents compact-model router collapse.
