##### **Online Temporal Rotations for 2 Bit KV-Cache Quantization in Video Diffusion Transformers**
2611.26334v1 by Ryan Lucas, Petros Prastakos, Rahul Mazumder

Video diffusion transformers reuse key--value (KV) caches across denoising steps, which quickly becomes a bottleneck for long video generation. We find that quantizing temporally distant cache vectors leads to a large range and high quantization error, but that nearby video tokens in the KV-cache share a persistent pattern per channel. These patterns also vary across videos, motivating rotations that are adapted to each video's unique cache. We propose Temporal Rotation Quantization (TRQ), a method that rotates frames in the cache that are adjacent in time before quantization. For each block of temporally adjacent cache vectors, TRQ fits an orthogonal transform that maps the block's per-channel means to a vector that is close to a constant across channels. To keep the quantization format lightweight, we parameterize this rotation as a tree of $2 \times 2$ planar rotations, each stored with only two bits, followed by a fixed Hadamard transform shared across all blocks. The rotations are fit without gradients or changes to model weights, and we prove that a simple greedy fit is globally optimal for our 'mean-flattening' objective. The transforms are fit online for each new video, and we release fused Triton kernels to make this process efficient at inference. At 2-bit precision and matched compression, TRQ improves VBench subject consistency over the state-of-the-art method QVG from 90.38 to 92.02 on LongCat-Video-13B and from 86.33 to 87.61 on HY-WorldPlay-8B, as well as improving other metrics.

##### **What If Chain-of-Thought Is Not Enough?**
2611.26282v1 by Marc Jansen

Reasoning capabilities in large language models have evolved from prompting-based methods to internalized model behaviors. Instead of relying on Chain-of-Thought, we train models to follow Question--Option--Criteria (QOC), a structured decision process that decomposes a task into explicit questions, competing options, and evaluation criteria. ##### **dLoop: Looped Diffusion Language Models via Recurrent Latent Refinement**
2611.26275v1 by Handa Li, Pengxiang Li, Mingyu Cao, Xilu Wang, Li Shen, Da Li, Lu Yin

Diffusion language models (DLMs) support parallel generation, yet they often require many denoising steps because only a fraction of token predictions are sufficiently reliable to be committed at each step. Existing acceleration methods primarily optimize confidence learning or commitment policies. However, these approaches operate on the outputs of a single denoising forward pass, in which information from the prompt, previously committed tokens, and uncommitted positions may not yet be fully integrated. ##### **Similarity-based OSF Logic for Flexible SPARQL Query Answering**

We present an updated version of fosf, a reasoner for fuzzy order-sorted feature (OSF) logic, with support for flexible SPARQL query answering over RDF knowledge graphs. Fuzzy OSF logic is a knowledge representation and reasoning language where feature symbols denote functions and sort symbols denote fuzzy sets. Sorts are organized in a fuzzy subsumption relation that extends to OSF terms, record-like structures representing classes of entities. The unification algorithm for these structures provides a calculus of fuzzy type subsumption. Existing methods employ globally uniform operations, potentially overlooking regional differences and causing incomplete restoration or even damage to image content. To address these challenges, we propose RegionAgent, an adaptive restoration framework that trains a Vision-Language Model (VLM) through two-stage preference learning to generate a region-specific restoration action at each iteration. Specifically, we first train the VLM to select an action suited to the current region through step-level Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), using preferences derived from immediate restoration feedback to supervise only the differing component in each action pair. To account for the effects of current actions on subsequent restoration, we then apply trajectory-level DPO, using preferences derived from complete trajectory scores to supervise the sole differing action in each trajectory pair. Furthermore, as conventional image-pair datasets do not directly provide preferences over regional restoration decisions, we construct a regional restoration preference dataset comprising 10K action pairs and 20K trajectory pairs for two-stage VLM training. Experiments across multiple datasets demonstrate strong restoration performance under both mixed and single degradations. Ablation studies further validate the effectiveness of two-stage preference learning in improving restoration quality.

##### **Budget Internalization: Fixed-Budget RLVR Trades Test-Time Scaling for In-Budget Accuracy**
2611.26172v1 by Ilia Zavidnyi, Alexander Kovrigin, Arina Puchkova, Kirill Gelvan, Sergey Titov

Reasoning models often become more accurate when they can generate longer answers at test time. This behavior is known as test-time scaling. However, RL with verifiable rewards (RLVR) usually trains them with a fixed rollout cap. We find that this cap changes model behavior, not only training cost. After training, models use few additional tokens beyond the training budget. They outperform the base models near that budget, but their accuracy stops improving and falls below the base model at larger budgets. The rollout cap thus becomes a learned constraint, that limits model's test-time scaling. We call this effect budget internalization. We observe it across Qwen3.5-4B, Gemma4-E2B, and Nemotron-3-Nano-4B and two reasoning training tasks: mathematical reasoning and diverse, procedurally generated problems. This effect also transfers from math training to science and coding tasks. Training-objective level ablations on Qwen3.5-4B show that most changes recover little test-time scaling. Stronger KL regularization and lower training-data truncation preserve more scaling, but stronger KL reduces in-budget accuracy. Forcing an answer on truncated training rollouts largely removes budget internalization, but also removes most of the in-budget gain. SFT on artificially truncated traces reproduces budget internalization, whereas SFT on naturally short traces does not, which shows that the effect is not specific to RL. This result links budget internalization to length-censored post-training rather than to an RL-specific objective term. Thus, a rollout cap does not only control training cost. It can trade low-budget gains for lower performance when additional inference compute is available.

##### **The FrontierOR Challenge: Agentic Algorithm Design for Operations Research**
2611.26171v1 by Hai Wang, Bretislav Hajek, Chonghe Jiang, Minwei Kong, Ao Qu, Huatian Gong, Bo Dong, Cynthia Barnhart, Jinhua Zhao

Operations research (OR) relies on algorithms that experts tailor to each problem: a new routing, scheduling or network design problem can take weeks of specialist work before it is solved well at scale. Large language model (LLM) agents now write, test and improve code, but whether they can design algorithms that scale to realistic instances is open: in the FrontierOR benchmark's one-shot evaluation, the strongest model matches Gurobi, with an objective within 1% of Gurobi's and no slower, on only 39% of the large instances. The FrontierOR Challenge asks teams to build agentic workflows that design, test, and improve algorithms for many optimization problems at once. Stage 1 releases 60 of the benchmark's 180 audited problems, spanning six application categories and five formulation types, in weekly rounds of 15; Stage 2 introduces new problems outside the benchmark, with unseen instances. Two tracks are ranked separately: in the Main track we run the submitted algorithms in a network-isolated sandbox with open-source dependencies only; in the Self-hosted track teams solve the instances on their own machines, with any software, within a timed window. Every solution is checked for feasibility, and a feasible solution scores the ratio of its objective to a frozen known reference, averaged over hidden instances.

##### **Can World Models Predict Rice Growth? Toward Agricultural World Models**
2611.26145v1 by Wenkai Ji, Zeteng Li, Yinkun Guo, Jie Zhou, Liang He, Zhenong Jin

World models can imagine how a physical scene evolves over the next few seconds, but growers need to know how a crop will develop over weeks to months: will this rice plant have headed a month from now, and how will it look at harvest? We introduce agricultural world modeling, the task of predicting how the same plant will look at a requested horizon of days to months, from one photo of it, its cultivar, and its camera view. In preliminary analysis, even state-of-the-art image generators get this wrong: they often grow the plant too little or give it the color and form of the wrong growth stage. We argue that they do not work out the plant's developmental state, which the photo reveals only indirectly. We therefore propose AgroWM, which reasons about development before it draws growth. Its reasoning module, PhenoClock, works out the current growth stage, predicts the future one, and writes a Growth Script with numeric targets for height, plant area, and color; its generation module, the Rooted Renderer, then edits the photo to follow this script, adding new organs and colors to the photographed plant. Because pixel scores can prefer an unchanged copy of the photo, we instead check each forecast's traits and developmental timing against the plant's own later photos. On rice, 54.6% of the forecasts of AgroWM are matched to the target stage (Stage R@1), against 34.8% for GPT Image 2.5, 24.0% for Qwen Image 2.1, and 47.4% for the strongest domain-specific baseline, the same backbone fine-tuned without Growth Scripts; 9 to 20 weeks ahead, it still reaches 46.4%, against 35.7% for that baseline. On maize, a crop with a different body plan and stage scale, the same design again scores best on all four measures.

##### **ChronoPatch: Black-Box Energy-Latency Attacks on Vision-Language Models**
2611.26117v1 by Jiawei Xing, Li Wang, Xiangtao Meng, Chuanchao Zang, Rui Wen, Zheng Li

##### **Pareto-Optimal and Strategyproof Data-Sharing Between Competitors**
2611.26108v1 by Morgan Colin-Mourre, Felipe Garrido-Lucero, Patrick Loiseau

We study data-sharing among firms competing in the same market through the quality of their predictive models. We model firms' profit through two main components, precision and market share, where the former depends on the firm's own number of datapoints and the latter depends on the endowments of all firms. We characterize Pareto-optimal contracts as those in which at least one firm receives all of the other firms' datapoints. We then propose a parametric family of data-sharing contracts and show that, under any of them, the relative market shares of all firms remain constant, eliminating the negative effect of sharing data with competitors and equalizing all firms' gains relative to the no-sharing scenario. We further show that one contract within this family satisfies both Pareto-optimality and strategyproofness against firms attempting to under-declare the size of their initial endowment. Finally, using a real-world dataset, we empirically test the gain in profit for all firms under the contract described above, and show that data-sharing is particularly beneficial when the machine learning task is harder or when firms' initial endowments are closer to homogeneous.

##### **RL Can Only Reinforce What It Sees Succeed**
2611.26084v1 by Ruben Sanchez-Corcuera, Unai Zulaika, Aritz Bilbao-Jayo, Miguel Fernandez-de-Retana

When a familiar rule stops working, an agent has to weigh what pre-training taught it against what it now observes. When the right action is rare, each run either learns it everywhere or not at all, and which runs take off is predicted before training by one count: the expected number of groups of attempts in which the action wins. 2611.26018v1 by Diaoxuan He, Linqian Wu

We previously proposed a surrogate model that addresses this bottleneck by predicting the orientation field in about 3 minutes. This paper reports the implementation, validation, and operational design that turned the model into an engineering tool for Honda design workflows. We present staged validation from 2D plates to production parts, evaluation against a predefined engineering angular-error criterion, and the expert review that approved design use in March 2025; the system has since been transferred to the crash-safety group and is in initial, limited use in directed fracture-prediction scenarios. The developed system combines injection-gate setting support for cases in which gates are unknown (90% gate-count accuracy, mean position error 11% of flow length), voxelization, surrogate inference, automatic mapping to the crash CAE model, and report generation in a single GUI. The complete orientation-preparation workflow takes about 0.1 hours, a 99.6% reduction from the conventional 25-hour flow-analysis route, and can be run directly by crash CAE engineers without molding-specialist support. As evidence of practical impact, component-level crash analysis using the predicted orientation field reduced the absolute peak-force error from 19.0% with an isotropic baseline to 11.4%, while confining predicted fracture to the test-observed region. The deployment lesson is that a surrogate model alone is not enough for practical adoption: gate-setting support, automatic data conversion, and shareable reporting are what make the model usable as a design tool.

##### **Policy Improvement-Guided Replay for Continual Reinforcement Learning**
2611.25989v1 by Qin-Wen Luo, Haoxuan Gao, Sheng Ren, Xiang Chen, Sheng-Jun Huang

Continual reinforcement learning (CRL) requires models to acquire new capabilities while retaining performance on previous tasks. A stability-oriented perspective common in conventional continual learning emphasizes knowledge preservation and interference mitigation. Complementing this perspective, we revisit CRL for vision-language models through policy improvement, a fundamental principle of RL, to preserve acquired performance gains. Using the performance difference lemma, we characterize historical performance changes through the current policy's expected advantages on historical task distributions, motivating replay of historical prompts with the current policy. To allocate limited replay memory, we propose Contrastive Likelihood Replay (CoLiR), which contrasts likelihood gains on correct responses from the final task policy with likelihood reductions on incorrect responses from the initial policy. Our analysis links correct-response likelihood gains to reference-policy advantages under an idealized KL-regularized optimal update and provides an endogenous-reward interpretation for general policy updates. CoLiR estimates scores from finite training rollouts and cached log-likelihoods, selects prompts at task boundaries, and replays them uniformly during subsequent training, with KL regularization restricted to replayed prompts. Memory construction only requires a small amount of additional cross-policy scoring but no additional response generation. Across three Qwen3-VL model sizes on MRCL, CoLiR achieves the highest mean final accuracy and mean task accuracy among the compared methods while maintaining competitive new-task performance.

摘要：

##### **Law at the Speed of AI: Critical Legal Infrastructure for the Machine Age: Autonomy Under Law Architecture (AULA)**
2611.25985v1 by Natasha E Blycha, James Myint, Chen Feng, Dr Amani Soysa, Koshin Jama, Schellie-Jayne Price, Matthew Blycha

The global deployment of autonomous AI agents within and across organisations exceeds the governance infrastructure available to constrain it. Agent communication and authentication protocols, principally the Model Context Protocol and the Agent2Agent Protocol, are maturing quickly, but maturity at the protocol layer does not produce legal legibility at the governance layer. Runtime enforcement written by the deploying organisation fixes the resources an AI agent can reach, not the authority under which it acts. Legal legibility is the capacity of a legal system to recognise, interpret and regulate activity by mapping it onto legal personhood, property, rights and liabilities. Existing (analogue) legal infrastructure cannot supply it for autonomous deployment. The resulting deficit is bidirectional. It concerns the attribution of liability when an AI agent causes harm, and the allocation of rights, including data and economic rights, generated by agentic activity and by the AI agent itself as a source of value and form of property. This paper characterises that deficit as the legal attribution gap, a material and unaddressed risk to economies, organisations and citizens that is magnified in cross-organisational multi-agent deployment. The paper describes the Autonomy Under Law Architecture (AULA), in which AI systems and data assets are registered as legal objects anchored to one or more identified legal persons, governed by cryptographically tethered smart legal instruments and evaluated at runtime. Each smart legal instrument can carry a legal context clause, through which the legislation, regulation and judicial decisions in force at the time of the act are given practical and legal effect in that evaluation. Infrastructure of this kind is a precondition for the continued function of any legal, property and economic system with both human and autonomous machine stakeholders.

##### **Energy estimates of open-weight large language models across stated food weights: a dose-response evaluation**
2611.25976v1 by Donato Riccio, Fabrizio Maturo, Iulia Brezeanu

We tested whether energy estimates of open-weight large language models (LLMs) scale proportionally with an exactly stated food weight, and whether computing energy density times weight removes the error. Under a prespecified protocol, seven open-weight LLMs estimated the energy of 168 randomly drawn foods from the USDA Food and Nutrient Database for Dietary Studies at nine stated weights, from 0.25 to 4 times the default amount. The primary outcome was the elasticity of the estimate with respect to weight (1: proportional scaling). A two-step estimate multiplied each model's own stated energy density by the weight in code. All seven models compressed their estimates, with elasticities from 0.548 to 0.979 (every 95% CI below 1); four compressed severely (0.548 to 0.709). These four overestimated a quarter of the default amount by 47% to 130% and underestimated four times it by 20% to 34%. Supplying the density restored near-proportional scaling in three of the four, and the two-step estimate cut the typical error factor at these extremes from 1.47-2.43 to 1.20-1.38. Compression persisted at full precision and grew in five of seven models under a plain question. The models hold the energy densities needed for proportional scaling and answer near a typical serving, reproducing the flat-slope syndrome of dietary recall. Accuracy at usual portions does not reveal it. Food-logging tools should compute the energy of weighed entries as density times weight, and evaluations should test fixed foods across stated weights.

##### **The Depth of a Prediction: Where Language Models Resolve a Word Predicts Human Reading Time**
2611.25970v1 by Donato Riccio, Fabrizio Maturo

Surprisal from a language model's final layer predicts how long people read a word, yet the model computes its prediction across many layers. We read each word's prediction at every layer with the tuned lens and summarize this layerwise surprisal trajectory by its resolution depth, the depth around which its change is centered. On eight English eye-tracking and self-paced reading corpora (81,957 words) and ten GPT-2, OPT and Pythia models, words that a model resolves later, and the words after them, are read more slowly than equally surprising words. Resolution depth improves held-out prediction beyond final-layer surprisal, entropy and the best single layer by 0.26 millinats per word (pooled p = 0.001), about a third of entropy's gain, and each model family gains, GPT-2 and Pythia through one corpus each. The net gain varies between corpora (random-effects mean 0.07, p = 0.37); against layer-shuffled depth, which adds as many coefficients, it is positive in seven of eight corpora (random-effects mean 0.23, p = 0.016), and untrained networks show no gain under the logit lens. Pooled over corpora, the effect holds with corrected word probabilities and beyond first-to-last-layer divergences, and a whole-trajectory functional regression adds no significant information beyond resolution depth.

##### **When Sounds Turn Heads: Recovering Event Timing That Audio LLMs Fail to Say**
2611.25960v1 by Jongbhin Woo, Jaewook Jeong, Joon Son Chung

Audio LLMs can know when a queried sound occurs but fail to say it. We find a few attention heads that track the queried sound: they shift when the query changes, follow the sound when it moves, and removing or blocking them often hurts the model's timestamps. Yet the answer does not express what they track. Intervals read directly from these heads outperform the model's original answer and recover events that its timestamps miss. To bring this timing into the answer, we introduce head-guided decoding, which needs no training. It keeps the model's decision on whether the sound is present and rescores its candidate timestamps with a prior from these heads. Heads selected on development data transfer unchanged to new recordings and free-text queries, where head-guided decoding improves the timestamps of three audio LLMs, scores above their head readout on every benchmark, and, in most settings, outperforms random-head guidance. On recordings released after the models were trained, head-guided decoding raises mIoU from 0.07-0.36 to 0.48-0.56. These results suggest that poor temporal answers in audio LLMs partly reflect a failure to say rather than to hear, and that the model's own attention can close part of this gap.

##### **Web search in Latin America: language models use less local and diverse news than search engines**
2611.25955v1 by Francisco W. Kerche, Franziska Sofia Hafner, Felix M. Simon, Luc Rocher

AI platforms are becoming a major gateway to news and current events. This shift risks amplifying existing geographical biases that favour narratives from wealthier countries over those from the Global South. This is particularly relevant in Latin America, which accounts for the largest share of AI usage in the Global South. We examine how information about Latin America changes when users shift from conventional search engines to LLM-based search. We construct a dataset of 1,901 current events across nine Latin American countries, craft realistic queries, and submit them to three search engines and four language models. We vary query language and user location to represent local, diaspora, tourist, and foreign user scenarios. Across 49,207 requests and 8,834 unique domains, language models return a less diverse set of news sources than search engines. Across user types, the share of domestic news returned by language models is inconsistent. For local users, while search engines already return only 41.3\% local news, language models return a further 3.6 to 11.6 percentage points less. Taken together, our findings suggest that LLM-based search can reproduce and, in some settings, amplify geographical hierarchies in the reach and visibility of news sources. We release an open dataset to support multilingual and geographically diverse evaluation of LLM-based search, and highlight implications for more equitable information systems design.

##### **GaussTrans: A Gaussian Interface Framework for Multi-Layer Refractive Transport**
2611.25950v1 by Zixuan Zhao, Tao Wang

We investigate surface modeling of multi-transparent interfaces to achieve accurate light transmission. The interplay of errors in geometric structure, light paths, and observation results during the reconstruction of transparent media with a high number of layers makes this a challenging problem. Some existing techniques address the reconstruction of transparent scenes by reducing the number of layers or simplifying light paths; however, this limits their direct applicability to high-order transparent scenes. We propose GaussTrans, a compact framework for light transport in high-order transparent scenes, consisting of three components: Gaussian-Field Hybrid Surfaces, Transport-Coupled Refinement, and Primitive-Guided Implicit Intersection. Gaussian-Field Hybrid Surfaces use a hybrid approach combining Gauss surfaces and fields to reconstruct different interfaces. Transport-Coupled Refinement reduces coupling errors by evaluating the entire light path. Primitive-Guided Implicit Intersection combines primitive support regions to reduce errors in the intersection of interfaces and rays. In experiments on datasets such as RefRef, GaussTrans achieves significantly higher accuracy with relatively low spatial storage requirements.

##### **Fast-JEPA: Advancing the Success-Latency Frontier for Latent World Models**
2611.25927v1 by Yao Zhang, Bin Yu, Shijie Lian, Haishan Liu, Changti Wu, Yu Wu, Kai Chen

Latent world models predict the outcomes of action sequences, but comparing candidate plans through recursive rollout remains expensive at test time. We introduce Fast-JEPA, a latent world model planning framework that advances the success-latency Pareto frontier of predictive search. Fast-JEPA reorganizes predictive computation to support effective action-sequence search at low planning cost. It constructs a goal-directed plan to guide search and directly predicts the outcomes of complete candidates for efficient comparison, avoiding recursive state rollout during refinement. Across PushT, Reacher, Cube, and TwoRoom, Fast-JEPA achieves 96.50% average success rate with 2.41x faster planning than INTACT. Search-budget sweeps show that its competitive success and low latency extend across candidate counts and refinement depths. We further introduce DynaGoal, a benchmark for evaluating world-model planning under dynamic out-of-distribution conditions, execution deadlines, and controlled response delays. Fast-JEPA achieves average success rates of 73.83% on DynaGoal-Shift and 87.67% on DynaGoal-Deadline, demonstrating the practical value of low-latency predictive planning under changing control conditions and execution-time constraints. Our code is available at https://github.com/Sofilyzia/Fast-JEPA.

##### **ROAM-GRPO: Enforcing Reasoning Diversity in GRPO via Routed Off-mode Approach Mining**
2611.25926v1 by Jeff Duan, Matt Thomson

Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) paired with Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) has become the dominant recipe for post-training LLMs on complex reasoning tasks, but has been shown to collapse the policy into a low-diversity mode. Repeated LLM outputs are nearly identical, and pass@k accuracy converges or degrades as a result. We propose ROAM-GRPO (Routed Off-mode Approach Mining for GRPO), an augmentation to the GRPO algorithm which incentivizes exploration of distinct reasoning strategies and aims to increase output creativity during evaluation. ROAM-GRPO detects well-solved problems during training and transfers them to an "off-mode" track. The model is prompted to produce distinct solution strategies to such problems, which are verified for logical validity and distinctness by an LLM judge and subsequently self-distilled into the policy. Training Qwen3.5-2b on a 1,000-problem subset of the MATH lvl5 dataset, ROAM-GRPO implementation increases peak pass@16 by 4.7% and correct embedding cosine distance by approximately 1.5-2x on a difficult evaluation set.

##### **Many Solutions, One Answer: Search Order Shapes the Default Solutions of Language Models**
2611.25916v1 by Qinhan Hou, Jing Tang

When a constraint puzzle has several correct answers, language models tend to give the same one.We study four open-weight models from three families on symbolic puzzles and multi-solution ZebraLogic variants with exactly known solution sets.Repeated samples concentrate on one default solution far beyond uniform sampling, and different models often share it.Yet models usually produce even rarely sampled solutions when added clues uniquely identify them.On held-out symbolic puzzles, we compare visiting entities in name order with visiting those with the fewest initial candidates first. Both searches try values in the stated order. Where their pre-specified predictions differ, the latter matches a larger share of responses in all four models, by 26 to 40 percentage points.Reversing the stated value order or specifying a search order shifts responses towards the solution that the new order favours, on both testbeds. The largest observed validity losses are 3 and 4 percentage points, respectively. Randomised presentation broadens answers more than a random-choice instruction in each of the three main models.

##### **The Linguistic Structure of Dependency-Conditioned Embeddings**
2611.25909v1 by Bach Phan-Tat, Tung Kieu, Kris Heylen, Dirk Geeraerts, Stefano De Pascale, Dirk Speelman

Dependency-based distributional models capture syntactically informed lexical similarities, but the geometric organisation of dependency-conditioned lexical representations remains underexplored. We investigate the global and local geometry of lemma-dependency relation embeddings using four representations trained on WikiText-103: two count-based PPMI configurations and two Skip-Gram with Negative Sampling (SGNS) configurations. At the global level, we analyse dependency-relation centroids, control for lexical identity through within-lemma centring, and examine their alignment with part-of-speech (POS) distributions. Locally, broader lexical similarity structures are partially preserved across dependency relations, while immediate neighbourhoods vary substantially, highlighting different usages. ##### **From Cases to Criteria: Reusing Evaluation Knowledge for Task-Specific Rubric Generation**
2611.25903v1 by Qiyan Deng, Long-Kai Huang, Kangfei Zhao

Query-specific rubrics make LLM evaluation more fine-grained and transparent, and generating high-quality rubrics requires both identifying the target query's evaluation scope and specifying concrete checks. Historical rubrics provide reusable evaluation experiences, yet existing approaches reuse either complete query-rubric cases or aggregated criteria and dimensions without explicitly connecting recurring evaluation requirements to their criterion-level evidence and source-query context. This can preserve irrelevant task details or lose the broader evaluation structure. In this paper, we introduce RubricDuo, which organizes historical query-rubric pairs into a Rubric Knowledge Graph linking evaluation dimensions, shared concepts, local concepts, criteria, and queries, where reusable evaluation requirements across queries are abstracted by shared concepts. Given a target query, RubricDuo first combines concepts relevant to the target query with retrieved historical cases to draft a rubric with broad coverage. It then revisits each draft criterion, retrieves concept-linked historical criteria and their source queries, and adapts applicable evidence to refine the criterion. Experiments on RubricBench and HealthBench demonstrate that RubricDuo achieves the overall best performance among the evaluated automatic methods in both alignment with human-authored rubrics and downstream LLM judging.

##### **Verifiable Logic in Medical LLMs: A Critical Review of Agentic Clinical Reasoning Optimization and Neuro-Symbolic Grounding**
2611.25898v1 by Youssef ElNahas, Adel Ammar, Soumaya Chaffar, Wadii Boulila

Clinical large language models are usually judged by whether the final answer is right. Yet a model can reach a fluent, plausible output through invalid reasoning that static benchmarks do not detect, and confident hallucination, causally invalid inference, and spurious demographic shortcuts can pass conventional evaluation. This critical review covers four paradigms in turn: how clinical hallucination arises and why static metrics miss it; the agentic hallucination cascade, in which multi-agent and tool-augmented systems propagate and amplify early reasoning errors; process reward modeling as step-level supervision; and neuro-symbolic grounding, in which formal logic engines, ontology reasoners, and knowledge graphs place verifiable constraints on neural output. Within their encoded domains, systems with symbolic veto layers offer guarantees that hold relative to their encoded knowledge, and they report large gains on the safety measures they use. These results come from single studies, mostly in simulated or small-sample settings, and are not directly comparable with neural baselines. Process reward models remain bounded by the statistics of their training data. We propose a conceptual framework that maps each agentic failure mode to a symbolic intervention point, and we argue that clinical AI safety depends substantially on system architecture. Because current regulation emphasizes performance and does not require verification of reasoning steps, we argue that responsible certification should also audit reasoning trajectories alongside accuracy on static benchmarks.

2611.25896v1 by Edgar A. Duéñez-Guzmán, Eduardo Dueñez, Edgar Said Hernandez Sanchez, Suzanne Sadedin

Demanding a verifiable artifact from an LLM reduced the score from $93$ to $68$ of $120$ tasks ($77\%$ to $57\%$). #### Abstracts
##### **DeepQuest: From Knowledge Gaps to Scientific Discovery in Alzheimer's Disease**
2611.26343v1 by Chunbo Jiang, Yifei Chen, Beining Wu, Fuyou Mao, Weihong Chen, Enyu Bao, Mingxuan Liu, Feiwei Qin, Qiyuan Tian

Automating experiments does not necessarily advance scientific understanding. Research agents must identify meaningful knowledge gaps, determine what available data can resolve, and assess whether their findings advance existing knowledge. We introduce DeepQuest, a knowledge-gap-driven AI scientist framework for Alzheimer's disease research. DeepQuest transforms unresolved questions from domain reviews into traceable, testable hypotheses and prioritizes them through data feasibility screening and groupwise Elo ranking. It couples iterative experimentation with two-level auditing that separates experimental reliability from knowledge contribution relative to two frozen literature corpora. On OASIS-3, DeepQuest produces 42 feasible hypotheses and outperforms the strongest baselines in 14 of 15 evaluator-dimension comparisons across three language-model reviewers. The full framework achieves an audit-assessed scientific finding rate of 95.24%, outperforming both general-purpose executors and domain-specific research agents. This scientific finding rate remains stable across literature corpora and evaluator model families. Representative findings concern neuropsychiatric symptoms and dementia risk, longitudinal amyloid spatial stability, and neocortical tau-associated clinical impairment, with key associations supported in independent ADNI and AIBL cohorts. These results show how aligning knowledge gaps, data feasibility, and evidence auditing can turn automated research into traceable, evidence-supported scientific discovery. Our source code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/DeepQuest-3066/

摘要：

##### **Verifiable Logic in Medical LLMs: A Critical Review of Agentic Clinical Reasoning Optimization and Neuro-Symbolic Grounding**
2611.25898v1 by Youssef ElNahas, Adel Ammar, Soumaya Chaffar, Wadii Boulila

Clinical large language models are usually judged by whether the final answer is right. Yet a model can reach a fluent, plausible output through invalid reasoning that static benchmarks do not detect, and confident hallucination, causally invalid inference, and spurious demographic shortcuts can pass conventional evaluation. This critical review covers four paradigms in turn: how clinical hallucination arises and why static metrics miss it; the agentic hallucination cascade, in which multi-agent and tool-augmented systems propagate and amplify early reasoning errors; process reward modeling as step-level supervision; and neuro-symbolic grounding, in which formal logic engines, ontology reasoners, and knowledge graphs place verifiable constraints on neural output. Within their encoded domains, systems with symbolic veto layers offer guarantees that hold relative to their encoded knowledge, and they report large gains on the safety measures they use. These results come from single studies, mostly in simulated or small-sample settings, and are not directly comparable with neural baselines. Process reward models remain bounded by the statistics of their training data. We propose a conceptual framework that maps each agentic failure mode to a symbolic intervention point, and we argue that clinical AI safety depends substantially on system architecture. Because current regulation emphasizes performance and does not require verification of reasoning steps, we argue that responsible certification should also audit reasoning trajectories alongside accuracy on static benchmarks.

摘要：

##### **The Price of an Explanation: What Demanding Verifiable Artifacts Costs, and What It Buys Back**
2611.25896v1 by Edgar A. Duéñez-Guzmán, Eduardo Dueñez, Edgar Said Hernandez Sanchez, Suzanne Sadedin

Generative models produce many candidate solutions but provide no intrinsic signal by which to rank them. One mitigation is to demand a \emph{verifiable artifact}: a program that can be executed against the available evidence. A common concern is that requiring executable verification incurs costs in both solution quality and computational budget. We measure these two costs on the $120$-task ARC-AGI-2 evaluation set. Demanding a verifiable artifact from an LLM reduced the score from $93$ to $68$ of $120$ tasks ($77\%$ to $57\%$). Of those $25$ tasks, $21$ were reached by no program-producing configuration we ran, at any budget, by sampling or by search; yet for $27$ of the $28$ tasks that only the answer-only arm solved, some program arm held a program that reproduced every demonstration and still failed the test. The bottleneck is obtaining an explanation that \emph{generalizes}, not finding one. A short evolutionary search over two already-sampled programs, at no additional model cost, beats best-of-$2$ sampled code by $9.2 \pm 1.4$ tasks, and over a single sampled program beats $1$-shot sampling by $9.9 \pm 1.3$. Moreover, because a program can be checked against the training examples, any program-producing protocol can terminate early once a task is explained: under the same stopping rule, best-of-$n$ sampling declines up to $38\%$ of its budget and the evolved arm up to $49\%$, whereas an answer-only baseline cannot terminate early. Applying the same methodology to decoder synthesis for quantum error correction yields interpretable decoders evaluated against a certified panel of published decoders on held-out exam shots.

摘要：

##### **Explainable AI for Solar PV Power Forecasting: From Model Selection to Trustworthy, Reproducible Deployment**
2611.25894v1 by Nuzhat Khan, Muhammad Paend Bakht, Ab Al-Hadi Ab Rahman, Fabiano Pallonetto, Amy Fahy, Mohamed Khalafalla Hassan, Paul Stynes, Muhammad Anique Aslam, Terence O Donnell, Mohammad Riadh

Machine learning (ML) is now a standard tool for forecasting the output of solar photovoltaic (PV) plants, yet published accuracy figures are often hard to compare and harder to trust. Many studies validate with random splits, report no reference forecast, and present feature attributions without saying what they can and cannot show. This paper reviews the main families of ML methods for PV power forecasting and resource assessment, from tree ensembles to deep and foundation models, and sets out a reproducible workflow that combines automated model search with explainable AI. The workflow is then tested on an openly available dataset: a 16.32 kWp plant in Porto, Portugal, forecast one day ahead from a 4 km numerical weather prediction grid. Over a full test year, the best models reach a normalised root mean square error (nRMSE) of 11.6% of installed capacity, about half the error of smart persistence. The choice of input features changed accuracy far more than the choice among strong algorithms, and shuffled cross-validation was mildly optimistic. After conformal calibration, the nominal 80% prediction intervals covered 81.7% of observations, against 64.5% before. SHAP rankings stayed stable when the model was retrained on different periods but shifted when correlated inputs were removed, so attributions should be read as properties of a model rather than of the plant. The paper closes with practical lessons and open problems, including deployment on edge hardware and the effect of a changing climate.

摘要：

##### **Coherent belief and opinion propagation produces more echo chambers: corrected version**
2611.25879v1 by Hiro Kataoka, J{é}r{ô}me Euzenat, Koji Hasebe

The occurrence of echo chambers is a well-known phenomenon in social networks. Echo chambers are groups of agents satisfying three properties: homogeneity, i.e., sharing the same opinion, segregation, i.e., listening primarily to agents of the same group, and reinforcement, i.e., increasing opinion similarity over time. Opinion dynamics models have been used to explain how echo chambers occur through agents updating their opinions. However, social network users also exchange beliefs supporting their opinions. Here, we aim at identifying the effect of propagating beliefs together with opinions on the formation of echo chambers. For that purpose, we complement an existing opinion dynamics model with classical belief merge operations. Beliefs and opinions are also maintained coherent guided by values that agents share. We introduce measures, applying to either beliefs and opinions, to identify echo chambers based on the three properties. This model is used to simulate opinion and belief propagation with varying initial conditions and agent tolerance for different opinions/beliefs. Results confirm the occurrence of echo chambers observed in previous opinion dynamics studies in a different setting and with a stricter measure. They also demonstrate the occurrence of echo chambers with belief propagation. Finally, they show that connecting opinions to beliefs and maintaining their coherence increases the number of belief echo chambers, but that they involve a reduced number of agents.

摘要：

##### **Anchor and Check: Deterministic Cross-Prompt, Cross-Model Reconciliation for Auditable LLM Decisions**
2611.25818v1 by Sivakameswara Reddy Chejarla

Pipelines that let a large language model (LLM) make discrete decisions need to know which decisions they may act on unattended, and a record that explains each one. We study a small, fully specified reconciliation contract: commit the target model's greedy answer (the anchor), draw one or two greedy checks that reach the answer by a different route (an alternate prompt, or a model from another family), and let a pure function return one of five outcomes, of which only Agree is acted on. On 48,000 model-item evaluations over 6,000 questions, with every method given access to verbalized confidence as a tie-breaker, the main finding concerns where calls come from: pooling another family's samples raises semantic entropy's AUROC by 1.7 to 4.0 points at every budget, and a same-model temperature check is the weakest design. As complete pipelines, the point estimates of the three-call contract and pooled semantic entropy over 3 or 5 samples differ by less than one point, with intervals containing zero. As detectors of the anchor's errors, the contract is ahead of pooled entropy with 3 and 5 samples under both tie-breaker conventions; with 10 samples the point estimates differ by less than one point and the sign depends on the convention. Under the strict Agree gate it automates 70% of items and still acts on 16% of errors. What the contract adds is a fixed committed answer, an explicit outcome and a decision replayable from its record; generation itself was not reproducible on the hosted API.

摘要：

##### **From Reliance to Overreliance: Taxonomizing and Detecting Relational Risks in AI Companionship**
2611.25734v1 by Viet Cuong Nguyen, Fiona Li, Chang Wan, Sheyril Agarwal, Nhi Nguyen, Angel Hsing-Chi Hwang, Munmun De Choudhury

Companion AIs are increasingly used for emotional support, yet distinguishing meaningful engagement from harmful reliance remains difficult. We introduce the Taxonomy of Relational Risks (TRR), which operationalizes users' emotional overreliance (EO) as observable conversational behaviors and separately identifies evidence of relational harm (RH), enabling empirical examination of when reliance behaviors co-occur with adverse consequences. Using naturalistic Character.AI and Replika conversations from 110 adults, we found EO-related behaviors in 63.2% of non-roleplay conversations, while observable relational harms appeared in 10.5%. Although EO did not necessarily lead to harm, recurring patterns often co-occurred with harmful outcomes, suggesting value as risk indicators. Open-source LLMs satisfactorily detected 12 of 14 EO behaviors in at least one configuration. Qualitative analysis revealed expectations of care, repeated support seeking, dependency, distress, and reduced confidence in personal judgment. We argue for graduated safety interventions tailored to patterns and trajectories of EO-related behavior.

摘要：

##### **An Explainable and Scalable Hierarchical Tensor Framework for Integrating Multi-Omics and Digital Pathomics**
2611.25584v1 by Ahmad Hussein, Ali Anaissi, Mukesh Prasad, Ali Braytee

Background: Precision oncology requires integration of heterogeneous molecular and pathology data, yet many fusion models offer limited biologically grounded interpretability and struggle with missing modalities.
  Methods: We present Hierarchical Interpretable Tensor Fusion (HIT-Fuse), integrating six modalities: somatic copy number variation, DNA methylation, RNA sequencing, microRNA expression, RPPA protein abundance, and vision transformer-derived pathomics across four TCGA cohorts (glioma, lung adenocarcinoma, breast, and kidney cancer). Modality-specific autoencoders compress high-dimensional inputs, followed by two-stage CP tensor decomposition. Missing latent modalities are reconstructed using clinical covariates and observed omics to expand cohorts and retrain downstream models. A mathematical traceback mechanism enables global and local xAI attributions.

Of decoded miRNAs, 71-92% have miRTarBase regulatory support, while 77-84% of selected genes are covered in DepMap.

##### **Why Do LLMs Help with Scientific Misconduct Requests and What to Do About It?: Increasing the Trustworthiness of AI for Science**
2611.25492v1 by Almene De Meran Meguimtsop, Daniel E. Acuna

Large language models (LLMs) support scientific research, but some of that assistance can also undermine research integrity. Existing safety evaluations in science often measure whether models refuse misconduct requests, but rarely explain whether they even recognize the problem or why they comply. We investigate this gap across 8 LLMs using SciIntBench, an existing benchmark of 1,350 prompts about scientific research integrity. We examine the reasons behind compliance in four steps. First, when asked directly whether fulfilling a request would involve misconduct, models overwhelmingly identify such requests, even in the Most Covert cases (91%), while they refuse to carry out the request in only 34% of cases. Second, we examine which request characteristics are associated with refusal using features that capture how the request is framed, how much the assistant has been told about what it is helping to hide, and how the goal and the user's stake are stated, among others. Third, we estimate that the most significant effect was to rephrase requests as questions about permissibility rather than instructions, which increased refusal from 52% to 88%. Finally, we test two system-prompt fixes: asking models to assess the request before responding and reminding them of responsible conduct of research principles. These changes increase refusal rates on adversarial requests from 58% to 87% and 86%, respectively. Our findings show that models recognize wrongful requests but still help users violate integrity. Understanding why these failures occur and testing ways to reduce them offer promising paths toward more trustworthy AI for science.

##### **How Reward Gain Imbalance Shapes Ranking: Rethinking Rubric-Based Reward Aggregation**
2611.24005v1 by Zhengyuan Fan, Zhonghua Wu, Tianyu Cheng, Qun Chen

Rubric-based reinforcement learning constructs rewards for aligning large language models with human preferences, typically by equally aggregating continuous scores that a judge assigns to individual criteria. Existing work has focused on improving judges, learning criterion weights, or refining aggregation, whereas the interaction between scoring heterogeneity and ranking remains insufficiently understood. We formalize this heterogeneity as \emph{reward gain imbalance}: each criterion enters the aggregated reward with its own reward gain, the gap between its expected scores on satisfied and unsatisfied responses. Counterintuitively, our experiments show that removing this imbalance, even with ground-truth labels, lowers ranking accuracy. To explain this, we derive an exact decomposition of the reward difference between any two candidates into a base satisfaction signal, a reward gain imbalance term, and a residual term, showing that reward gain imbalance can attenuate or amplify the base signal, and finding that misrankings usually concentrate on pairs where it attenuates. Guided by this analysis, we propose the adaptive \textbf{D}ual-\textbf{C}enter \textbf{C}lipping aggregation (DCC), which bounds each criterion's contribution to the reward difference instead of simply removing the imbalance. Using only the scores of the current candidate group, it determines a clipping interval that imposes a common upper bound on per-criterion score differences between candidates, limiting the excessive influence of extreme scores while preserving continuous judgment information....

##### **DL101 The Representation Capability of Neural Network Models**
2611.24980v1 by Fernando Berzal

Universal approximation theorems (Cybenko, Funahashi, Hornik & Leshno) provide the mathematical justification for why neural networks are powerful enough to model complex, nonlinear phenomena. Shallow networks can approximate any continuous function on compact sets. Leshno's theorem clarified that non-polynomial activations are necessary for universality, which explains why ReLU, logistic, hyperbolic tangent, and other common activations work (but pure polynomials do not).
  However, approximation theorems provide no efficiency, training, nor generalization guarantees. They do not indicate how many neurons are needed for a given accuracy, whether gradient descent will find the right parameters, or whether networks trained on finite data will generalize well.
  Barron provided quantitative error bounds for particular function classes (approximation rates for functions with bounded Fourier norm), whereas Montúfar was the first to show that expressivity grows exponentially with depth (in terms of the number of linear regions). Deep networks are strictly more expressive than shallow ones with the same number of parameters. Depth is fundamentally more powerful than width, providing a rigorous mathematical explanation for the use of deep learning models.

##### **Anticipating or Speculating? Making Futures Pay in AI Ethics**
2611.22268v2 by John P. Nelson

Philosophers have recently been trying to develop uniform decision rules for instances in which physicians' judgments diverge from the recommendations of medical artificial intelligence (AI) systems. I argue that this effort is fatally flawed because the performance and appropriate use of algorithmic recommendation systems by physicians are highly context-specific, empirical medical matters rather than epistemological ones. In such instances, general decision rules can be stipulated only to the extent that they abstract away from relevant, indeed determinative, contextual circumstances. The alleged problem of physician-AI disagreement, and others like it, play into longstanding disputes in technology ethics about the viability and utility of envisioning possible futures: is this useful "anticipation" or wasteful "speculation"? I suggest that the two may be distinguished. Anticipation explores plausible futures to help inform present actions under irreducible uncertainty. Speculation abstracts away uncertainty, developing formal solutions to stipulated circumstances whose relationship to the real present is often quite unclear. Efforts to develop crisp philosophical solutions to physician-AI disagreement, among other problems, fall into the latter category. Technology ethics should remember that fruitful futures thinking must both begin from and return to the messy, uncertain present.

Every tool output is written as a card that can be checked against the raw input; an independent checker that sees only the input and the cards decides which cards become evidence; and the reasoner answers from the admitted cards alone, with no training anywhere. On medical question answering, fundus glaucoma screening, and chest X-ray pneumonia classification, it achieves the highest accuracy among models of comparable scale, and significantly outperforms state-of-the-art tool-using agents and multi-agent systems built on the same backbone at lower cost.

##### **Optimistic Verifiers Get Hacked: Reward Hacking Under an Exact Process Oracle**

##### **Programmable Semantic Interventions: Learning Causal Carriers from Behavioral Programs**
2611.21307v1 by Paolo Muratore, Mackenzie Weygandt Mathis

Causal abstraction explains a frozen network by aligning its activations with the variables of a simpler, interpretable model, and checking that interventions on the two agree. Finding such an alignment currently requires a counterfactual dataset: for every pair of prompts, the answer the network ought to give once the variable has been changed. Here, we develop an approach that lets the investigator define that relationship directly: the answers the variable can produce, and which of them an intervention should yield. We call this interface a semantic program and introduce Programmable Semantic Interventions (PSI): a low-rank activation subspace learned so that interchanging it changes the model's answer as the program declares, with the expected answers read from the frozen model itself. Every medication comes with two types of medical facts: indications, which describe what conditions the drug treats (benefits), and contraindications, which describe who must avoid it to prevent serious harm (safety limits). Although contraindications are critical for patient safety and FDA regulations require listing them as rigorously as indications, we find that contraindications are far less documented than indications in general prose. In this paper, we measure how often these texts contain both facts across 574 drugs from PrimeKG by comparing a drug's indications against its own contraindications. Across Wikipedia articles and PubMed abstracts, we find that reference prose names a drug's benefits about twice as often as its safety limits (within-drug odds ratio 2.19). We call this documentation gap LABEL (Literature Asymmetry between Efficacy and Limits). Language models mirror this gap. As we scale Qwen2.5 from 0.5B to 14B, accuracy on indications rises by 18.3 points, whereas accuracy on contraindications remains flat near random guessing. However, when we provide models with a retrieved passage that states the answer, they use safety facts just as effectively as benefits. Furthermore, routing safety questions to official FDA labels boosts accuracy by 15.8 points whenever the label states the fact. Overall, our findings show that models fail on medication safety not because they cannot reason about risk, but because free-text corpora lack safety facts. Clinical AI systems should therefore route medication-safety questions to official regulatory labels and structured databases rather than relying on general public records. Code and data are available at https://github.com/SMRayeed/LABEL.

##### **From Sensors to Silicon: Causal Cross-Layer Self-Healing Edge AI for Secure and Energy-Adaptive IoT Systems**
2611.19165v1 by Mostafa Darvishi

The migration of artificial intelligence from centralized cloud infrastructure to resource-constrained Internet-of-Things (IoT) devices reduces communication latency and improves data locality, but it also tightly couples sensing quality, inference behavior, microarchitectural activity, hardware reliability, security, and energy management within the same physical node. Existing edge-intelligence solutions predominantly address these dimensions in isolation, making it difficult to determine whether an observed degradation originates from sensor malfunction, data-distribution shift, malicious execution, hardware faults, or resource and thermal stress. This paper presents a causal cross-layer self-healing Edge-AI framework that continuously observes synchronized telemetry spanning sensor health, inference uncertainty, processor hardware-performance counters, accelerator and memory activity, power and thermal state, and communication conditions. A temporally informed causal reasoning engine estimates the most probable origin of abnormal behavior and quantifies diagnostic uncertainty, including under concurrent perturbations.

##### **Effects of Conventional, Adaptive, and AI-Powered Upscaling on the Legibility of License Plates**
2611.18949v1 by Hany Farid, Joshua D. Bucci, Emily A. Cooper

Small details in digital images -- a face, a street sign, a license plate -- are routinely upscaled in hopes of making them interpretable. Upscaling options now range from conventional interpolation, whose operations are spatially invariant and fully explainable, to AI-powered methods that synthesize detail potentially absent from the source. Here, we ask which of these techniques most reliably helps human readers. In two online experiments, we measured legibility of 255 US license plates ranging in resolution from 128 to 8 pixels tall, as well as their legibility after upscaling by eight different methods. Legibility of the un-upscaled plates collapsed from ceiling to floor across a narrow band of plate heights (32 to 10 pixels). Conventional upscaling techniques (bilinear, bicubic, and Lanczos interpolation) gave modest gains of about 5-9 percentage points within this range. Nearest-neighbor interpolation, however, was harmful, rendering unreadable many plates that were readable without any upscaling. Advanced methods diverged: SwinIR, an open-source AI enhancer, raised performance by up to 38 points relative to no upscaling, while an adaptive denoiser-based image prior and commercial AI upscaler tended to impair legibility. We found no strong evidence of consistent character hallucinations across methods. Decomposing the task, conventional interpolators mainly helped readers count characters, whereas SwinIR alone made the characters themselves more identifiable.

In this work, we study Continual Recursive Self-Improvement (CRSI), a setting for continuing existing harness-optimization methods across compatible benchmarks or policy models. We propose Self-Improvement Memory (SIM) for the harness optimizer, rather than the task-solving policy. ##### **Dancing Tokens on the Vocabulary Network from LLM Generation**
2611.18248v1 by Yuan-Chao Hu

Large language models (LLMs) generate text one token at a time, yet how a particular generation relates to the geometry learned in pre-training remains unclear. The fundamental explainability of LLMs is elusive. Here, we build a similarity network from the input embeddings of six open LLMs and map generation onto it. The token-embedding network is scale-free while heavy-tailed, as are random embeddings with matched covariance, but far more fragmented. Distillation barely changes this topology because input embeddings are largely inherited. The knowledge space is thus deterministic from pre-training. Consecutive generated tokens are rarely graph neighbours and, in most models, no more often than in shuffled order, so generation is not a walk on the predefined network, although the tokens it visits are concentrated within it. Through the logit lens in a layer- wise manner, tokens settle late in depth in most models, and repetitive models remain confined to small domain-specific sub-graphs. These observables could serve as inference-time monitors of degeneration. We thus provide a fresh perspective on understanding LLM generation in the microscopic way from node or link prediction by graph learning.

##### **The Transformer Paradigm: Dynamic Processing and a New Principle of Inference**
2611.17953v1 by Marco Giunti, Fabrizia Giulia Garavaglia

We argue that the Transformer revolution is not adequately understood as a merely quantitative expansion of deep learning, but as the emergence of a new paradigm based on a different principle of inference. We interpret the Transformer as a system in which token representations are transformed by parameterized transformations, or transforming concepts. During inference, some of these transformations are dynamically constructed from the current input: previously acquired transformations are first adapted and then applied. At the mechanical-structural level, this dynamic processing is realized through output-weight interconnections, whereby outputs of some networks determine the weights of others. The resulting mode of processing, which we call Sequence-level Interactive Dynamic Parallel Processing (SIDPP), provides a framework for explaining strong prompt sensitivity and opens research directions concerning interpretability, control, alternative AI architectures, neuromorphic systems, and possible biological realizations of dynamic processing.

As Artificial Intelligence (AI)-based systems have become increasingly integrated into various aspects of everyday life, including education, understanding how these systems make decisions is of paramount importance. However, the opacity of AI-based decision-making in education (especially with deep learning and generative AI models) can hamper decision-making, especially when outcomes appear unjustified or incorrect. In this chapter, we argue that three key principles (uncertainty, explainability, and human responsibility) are essential for the legitimate use of AI in educational settings. Acknowledging the inherent uncertainties in AI-based decisions, analogous to those in human decision-making, is fundamental. Explainability is critical for ensuring that the functioning and outcomes of AI applications are transparent and comprehensible to both educators and learners. Furthermore, human responsibility is crucial, particularly in human decision-making, which remains indispensable across all stages of AI development and deployment. By examining these three principles, this chapter provides a comprehensive guide for stakeholders seeking to integrate AI thoughtfully and effectively into the educational system.

##### **From Data to Wisdom: Rethinking AI Through an Epistemic Lens**
2611.16726v2 by Afonso Eduardo

This paper proposes the Data-to-Wisdom Epistemic Architecture for AI (EAAI), a conceptual framework treating Data, Information, Knowledge, Understanding and Wisdom as five distinct but recursively interacting epistemic functions: observation, contextual interpretation, modeling and prediction, critical evaluation and informed judgment. Two observations motivate it: sequential readings of the Knowledge Pyramid obscure how these functions interact, and a targeted review of twelve Bayesian reference books finds that prior distributions are defined mathematically while "prior information" and "prior knowledge" are not consistently distinguished as epistemic categories. A Bayesian interpretation relates the likelihood, prior and posterior distributions, posterior prediction, model checking and decision analysis to the five functions, without limiting EAAI to Bayesian systems. Extending "prior" in an epistemic sense, the paper distinguishes five kinds of prior context that can shape the model, the likelihood, the prior, evaluation and action. Three worked applications -- quantitative financial risk, actuarial modeling in health insurance and agentic AI for medical reimbursement claims -- show how EAAI structures the analysis of model assumptions, evidence interpretation, validation, dependencies and decision consequences, separating the prediction of a risk, the appraisal of its reliability and the justification of action. It draws implications for AI and machine learning, including evaluation by function, treating intermediate model outputs as evidence open to challenge and separating generation from appraisal, and compares EAAI with work on DIKW, Bayesian workflow, AI evaluation and risk governance. Its contributions are an epistemic vocabulary with recursive relationships, a more accessible reading of Bayesian methodology, a typology of prior context and clearer methodological terminology

##### **An Energy-Based Perspective on Machine Learning**

This tutorial has a broader goal: beyond introducing EBMs, it uses them as a single lens that connects the different aspects of machine learning. Any EBM is specified by three choices, the energy, which determines what the model represents; the inference procedure, which samples from or optimizes the energy; and the learning method, which fits the energy to data. We first develop these components, including sampling, variational inference, and learning by maximum likelihood, noise contrastive estimation, pseudolikelihood, score matching, and adversarial training, and show that the learning methods share one contrastive structure. This view explains existing methods, organizes them into a common framework, and suggests new algorithms by replacing one component while keeping the others, with composition of energies as a principled way to combine learned models at inference time.

Retrieval accuracy varies substantially across updates, ranging from near-native performance to large losses. With source caches already available, reuse achieves median time-to-first-token speedups of $2.10$--$5.08\times$ on three longer-prefix QA benchmarks. To characterize the behavioral differences behind these outcomes, we introduce Functional Cache Divergence, which compares next-token distributions under reused and native caches. Direct KV cache reuse better preserves native model behavior in natural-text continuation, whereas compatibility degrades with increasing prefix length in single-token retrieval tasks. Answer-margin analyses help explain how correct retrieval decisions survive substantial internal state changes. Yet even agreement on the first generated token does not ensure agreement in answer content or stopping behavior. These findings establish task utility and fidelity to native inference as distinct criteria for evaluating cross-version KV cache reuse.

##### **Interleaving anatomical inspection and diagnostic reasoning for explainable radiology report generation**
2611.14018v1 by Chentao Huang, Runlong Lin, Zhijun Wang, Yunxiang Li, Yuanyuan Sun, Jian Wang, Hongfei Lin, Ling Luo

Automated radiology report generation can draft clinically plausible reports, yet the pathway from visual evidence to reported findings remains opaque, leaving the diagnostic basis of these reports unexplained and the generated reports difficult to review or revise. We introduce ARIVE (Anatomy-guided Reasoning with Interleaved Visual Evidence), an explainable report generation framework that interleaves anatomical inspection with diagnostic reasoning prior to final report composition. To support this process, we constructed 159,416 multimodal chain-of-thought (MCoT) trajectories from MIMIC-CXR, wherein visual evidence from inspected anatomical regions informs subsequent clinical interpretation. Trained on these trajectories and further optimized using anatomical and clinical-content rewards, ARIVE achieved superior clinical-efficacy performance compared with strong baselines on MIMIC-CXR and the abnormality-centered MIMIC-ABN. ARIVE's reports were competitive with those of leading models in blinded expert grading, and its MCoT trajectories earned high ratings for diagnostic traceability. Furthermore, a paired review evaluation suggests that access to MCoT can aid in identifying errors within generated reports.

##### **A Stage-Wise Explainable Pipeline for Arteriolar-to-Venular Ratio Estimation and Retinopathy Screening**
2611.14013v1 by Sharan Sharmeela Karuppannan, Yoga Sri Varshan, Subin Sahayam, Umarani Jayaraman

The arteriolar-to-venular diameter ratio (AVR) is a clinically motivated marker of retinal vascular health. We present a five-stage pipeline for AVR estimation and exploratory retinopathy screening from fundus images, with inspectable intermediate outputs at each stage. The pipeline combines vessel and artery-vein masks, optic-disc localization, vessel-calibre estimation using a minimal-chord boundary walk, and AVR computation from dominant arterial and venular branches. An explicit threshold-based rule produces the screening decision, enabling errors to be traced to individual processing stages. On an exploratory cohort of 30 HRF images, we report 96.7% accuracy, an F1-score of 0.968 and ROC-AUC of 0.978. Ablation studies and an exploratory comparison with VascX examine the effects of vessel pairing, calibre measurement and aggregation. Interpretation is limited by the small cohort, proxy disease labels and absence of independent reference calibre measurements. The pipeline provides a transparent framework for investigating retinal vascular biomarkers, with further validation needed on larger, clinically graded cohorts.

##### **The Case for AI-Enabled Gate Monitoring as Farm Security in the UK: A PRISMA-Guided Review of the Evidence on Rural Crime, Livestock Predation and Farm Access**
2611.13933v1 by Steve Symes, Paul Fergus, Mark Carr, Omar McIver, Arwyn Jones

Farm security in the UK rests on thinner evidence than its cost suggests. We ran a PRISMA guided review: twelve searches of Web of Science and Scopus on 2nd -4th November 2026 returned 1,183 records (761 unique), and grey-literature and hand-searches added 35 assessed sources. We synthesised 96 database records and 24 grey sources narratively. UK peer-reviewed evidence is small (16 records), and the main UK survey of crime and farmer mental health has 80 responses. Cost estimates rest on insurer claims: NFU Mutual put UK rural crime at £41.5 million in 2025, with livestock theft up almost 30% to about £4.5 million and dog attacks on livestock costing £1.95 million. The Home Office states that agricultural crime cannot be separately identified in its central data. We found no independent evaluation of cameras, sensors, trackers or marking; light and sound deterrents lost effect within one to five months in a systematic review of anti-predator interventions; and no study examined how often farm gates are left open or what follows. Camera-trap AI papers report accuracy on ecological datasets, none at a farm gate in the records we found, and in one comparison machine learning detected 41% of wildlife against 81% for analysts. We argue, as an inference from these gaps and not as a demonstrated effect, that continuous gate-level AI-mediated monitoring, as implemented in Fieldmark AI, would be highly beneficial for UK farm security. It would create the farm-level, time-stamped record that no official source holds, verify events that periodic human checks miss, resolve whether foxes, dogs or people were responsible, and give every other security measure something to be tested against. No study has yet shown that it reduces loss, and we set out the evaluation needed to show it.

##### **Beyond Proxy Metrics: MLLMs as Human Surrogates for Explainable AI Evaluation**
2611.13926v1 by Eoin M. Kenny, Salim I. Amoukou, Tom Bewley

Explainable AI (XAI) has expanded into a broad and impactful research area, but the fundamental question of how best to evaluate explanation techniques has proven frustratingly elusive. Methodologies typically revolve around either proxy metrics which tell us little about the utility of an explanation, or human evaluation which---while generally considered the gold standard---is expensive, difficult to scale, and challenging to reproduce. In this paper, we investigate if Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) can act as surrogate human participants to automate the evaluation of XAI. To do this, we first propose a ``purpose-grounded'' evaluation framework with metrics applicable to both MLLMs and human users alike. Then, we offer some conceptual insights as to why and when we can trust these models to automate this process. Lastly, we conduct extensive empirical testing across five purpose-grounded areas such as model improvement and fourteen popular MLLMs. Results indicate that all models trend positively, but OpenAI and Qwen models generally show higher alignment compared to other variants, which would be the recommended choices in practice. We expect our insights not to replace user testing, but rather to help XAI researchers complement their current evaluation methodologies and move the field away from heavy reliance on proxy metrics for measuring explanation utility.

##### **Softness is something you also need! Why compliance helps control: information metrics of a passive walker with compliant ankle**
2611.13779v1 by Fabio Bonsignorio, Elisabetta Barletta, Sorin Dragomir, Francesco Esposito, Enrica Zereik

A great gap in adaptivity, flexibility, and dexterity still exists between intelligent robots, whether they are made of rigid or (partially) compliant components, and their naturally evolved animal and vegetal counterparts. One reason could be of `paradigm' nature. On the one hand, most robots (both rigid and soft) are still designed by following a `Cartesian' approach, with top-down control and a clear mind-body separation. Designing artificial agents on the basis of the principles that we observe in biology is not an easy task. The principles of organization of animals and plants rely on morphological computation and emergence of fit behaviors from loosely coupled networks of embodied agents.
  Modeling and control require radically new approaches. In this paper we briefly recall and explain some seminal mathematical results linking information metrics and dynamics of the agents, already published years ago; we integrate them with some recent methods developed by us and by the community, we highlight some brief remarks about their relevance and we hint to our current and near future work towards finding concrete and efficient ways for the modeling and control of completely or partially compliant artificial agents and for a better understanding of physical intelligence in nature. We present a technical note summarizing phase-space volume calculations for inverted-pendulum and passive-dynamic walking models, following our proposed framework. Starting from the McGeer compass gait, we introduce knee-ankle compliance, foot-rocker effects, and a Ruina-style compass gait with distributed leg inertia. Standard heel-strike maps are derived and used in the estimation of viable phase-space volumes $W(γ)$, based on a state-dependent Monte-Carlo and Reproducing Kernel Hilbert Spaces methods. We show how the methods based on RKHS allow deeper and more subtle analysis.
